COUNTY OF CHESTER, TO WIT.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GEN- ERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said county, on MoNnAY, the Sad day of July next, at half-past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empan- nelled.

The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddisbury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 2nd day of July next, at half-past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Court House, Nether Knutsford, on WEDNFSDAY, the 4th day of July next, at eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when another Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow,Maccles- field, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed for the last- mentioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 4th clay of July next, at eleven o'clock in the Fore- noon.

REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. ettester, 11th June, 1894.

"CAPITAL" CHAT..

A true story has just arrived fmti India about a Bombay Parsee offering to buy thti Boa&c ia. The Parsee went on board the flagship and asked to see the Admiral. Whan the latter heard the purpose of the visit he entered into the joke, and gave orders for the would-be purchaser to be shown over the vessel. After a long inspection and a good deal of consideration, the Parsee made a firm offer for the fiagship,armament and all : but Admirable Ken- nedy said it was not nearly enough, and so the negotiations fell through.

For pure snobbery and for the worst of had taste I do not think that a notice just posted in a fash- ionable West-end church could be beaten. This arrogant proclamation intimates that those who collect the offertory have peremptory instructions not to receive coppers, and adds that. those who cannot afford threepence are welcome to worship free. The mite of the widow is not wanted, nor her presence either, unless she is willing to humiliate herself and display her poverty before the crowded congregation.

Was it not Goldsmith who wrote a certain couplet about the loud laugh that ekieaks the vacant mind? There has been strong temptation to recall this *saying in the lobby on Tuesday, where Mr. Howard 'Vincent, has been noisily handing about as a splendid joke a little green-backed tract, which lie offers as Mr. Schnadhorst's latest. On the back it is entitled " What the Liberals have done." With the exception of an inserted addendum, which says that since the work went to press Ladas had won the Derby, all the pages are blank. Herein we get a large conception of Mr. Vincent's pretty infantile wit.

That one Englishman is worth three Frenchmen, and goodness knows how many Russians and other trivialities of that kind, has long been reluctantly admitted by the envious nations of the earth. This being so, it can hardly be gratifying to tie English vegetarian societies to learn that we stand ahead of the world as flesh eaters. But the fact remains. John Bull stands first, the French come second, the Germans, Russians, Spaniards, and Italians follow- ing in succession, and the vegetarians can hardly argue that the Briton is not as good a man as the Italian. And by this Iby no means wih to depre- cate the value of vegetables.

The Opposition leaders are not, after all, so sanguine that, the dissolution is to come very speedily. Lord Randolph Churchill is to start on a tour, within the next fortnight, which is expected to last twelve months, and it is no secret that his plans have been submitted to and approved by Mr. Balour and Mr. Chamberlain. Lord Randolph him- self fully intends being back in time to take an active part in the campaign that will accompany the general election. All the same, I should not be surprised if there turns out to be some truth in the rumour that the abandonment of Lord Randolph's candidature for Bradford will shortly be announce d

When they do agree in the French Chamber their I unanimity is wonderful. On Saturday a Bill was passed withput comment for an extraordinary credit. of over a million and a half francs to be chiefly devoted to reinforcing the French posts on the Congo colonies. A wondertul country is France. Such implicit contidon,o is s” in a new, Mini:- try that it can obtain a million odd francs without cointoeui; uu fiction CL pure bluster. In a ek or two the Ministry may he defeated be- cause the house lacks confidence that it can man- age apiece ot railway admixistration. Meanwhile, the Parisians believe that they are frightening. England, and England—in that aggravating cool 'way it has—is not tha least alarmed.

It needs to he explained for the I enefit of those to whom the magic title "Senior Wrangler" is sup- posed to repreent the highest academic achieve- ment that some ten years ago the subjects previ- ously included in the Mathematical Tripos were sparated into two parts. Success in the firq of these still confers the dignity of senior wrangler, though shorn of some of itsold glory; hut the second in which examination takes place a year later, con- fers ao popular lustre, although it involves profici- ency in mathematics so mur..,11 higher than the Wranglers Tripos that few beyond those aspiring to fellowships go in for it. It is in this, known by the prosaic and unimpo.King title of " Part 'Second,' that Miss Johnson, of Newnharn, alone of all com- petitors, male or female, is placed in the first rank. —Class 1, Division 1 In the first part of the tripos of last year Miss Johnson was placed between the fifth and sixth wranglers.

There was a show at Westminster on Tnesday afternoon, held by the Royal Horticultural Society, which deserves very special notice. Really it marked a new departure in pansy culture. The panay used to be gaite the favourite flower with fanciers, but lately it has lost nearly all its popu- larity except in a few parts of the North. Nor is the reason for this far to seek. Fanciers strove alter distinctness and contrast of markings. There was to be a clearly defined margin in the pansy, equally clear blotche., and an exact eye. As in the case of many other flowers similarly cultured, it did not occur to the fanciers that these things are not floral beauties hut floral uglinesses. The old kind of pansy—and perfect it is in its mistaken way— was excellently displayed in the collection of Mr. rye, of Catterall. Garstanp, who obtained a gold medal for his exhibit. Mr. A.. 1. Rowberr:y carried all before him with the new gind, and won prize after prize presented by the London Pansy Society. This new kind is technically called the " sprayed violetta." Its shape is not rigid, and suggests no- thing of Euclid ; and its colours—never startling, but colours to linger over with delight—flow into one another most charmingly. Now at length one begins to think that there is an artistic future for the pansy. All this suggests that floriculturists, who have generally been wrong in their aims since their art began, should go through a course of in- struction on gracefulness of .shape, on harmony of hues, and on the right use of contrasts.

Plovers' eggs have all but disappeared from the market, but at one West End poultereee shop they have been replaced by guillemots' eggs, which arz certainly not a common article of food. Guille- mots. of course, being sea fowls, are themselves un- eatable. but that is no reason why their eggs should not be good. Thee eggs, which are pear-ehaped, vary greatly as to their colour. The commonest are n pretty green-blue. with splotches of ruddy brown or black, but some with the same markings are nearly white ; while white, blue or light brown eggs without Markings at all are not unusual. Guil- lemots' eggs are used for commercial purposes, such as lining wines and preparing fancy leathers.

There is every prospect that the Conference at Leeds next weelz on the subject of the House of Lords will be an interesting and animated one. I understand that the advanced Radicals in the House of Commons are likely to attendin consider- able numbers with the object of securing the adop- tion of a thorough-going programme. The following resolution is to be moved by Mr. Labouchere and supported, probably, by Mr. Philip Stanhope and others :—" That this Conference, being of opinion that the House of Lords is useless, dangerous, and ought to be abolished, demands that with thit, ob- ject in view the (4overnment should introduce dur- ing the present session a bill or resolution provid- ing that in any case when hills pasied in the House of Commons have been rejected by the House of Lords, or amendments introduced into them by the House of Lords which are not accepted by the House of Commons, it shall be in the power of the House of Commons, by simple resolution, either ni the same session or in the same Parliament. up such bills in the form in which they shall einally have left the House of Commons to the Crown for the Royal Assent ; and this Conference pledges it- self to support the Governments by all the means inherent in self-governing people.' It will he seen that this goes far beyond the simple curtailment of the veto of the Upper House, and it is likely to lead to an animated debate.

I learn that a requisition will shortly be pre- sented to the Chancellor of the Exchsquer on the part of the Radical members of the House urging him to talc, ,rep 4 to secure the more speedy progress nf the Finance Bill, and thus save the time of the session from being wasted.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE, I am reminded of children's =Weer dresses by eeeing a becoming and useful frock of butcher-blue linen trimmed with embroidery, in red cotton on blue linen. The skirt is set in box-pleats into an embroidered band, and the square-fronted Eton jacket has a sort of sailor collar worked to match, the full sleeves being gathered into tight embroidered cuffs' while the blouse worn beneath the jacket is of thin cream linen, box-pleated, and fastened with little gilt buttons. For little girls who are yet in the " smock " stage, the linen frocks, with squatte-cut bodices showing chemisettes beneath, are still very popular, and the prettiest are pale blue or green linen with white cambric chemisettes and full sleeves embroidered in the colour of the over-dress.

Cherry Is the most notable colour in millinery as far as straw is concerned. Tabac brown is also a favourite. Tuscan and crinoline are used in com- bination, and very few hats are entirely of one colour, both black and brown having an insertion bend of the natural Tuscan straw, or of crinoline In a cream or 4crn tint. Sometimes the crown is also of this light straw, and hats of this desoriptibn require very little trimming, and have a smart effect. Round hat shapes, both with straight and curved brims, are being worn. These are made of coarse and fancy straws, and are trimmed in many different styles; some have a wreath of violets round the crown, with a stiff bow of black satin on one side of the front, others have a butterfly bow of black lace placed immediately in the centre, with some artificial flowers and small ostrich tips standing erect at the back. Hats are an under- taking at present for amateur milliners, as they need to be very neatly finished, while the elaborate trimmings are difficult to manage; this is not the came, however, with toque-shaped hats and bon- net.; the former are trimmed symmetrically with flowers and lace wings, while large bows of very wide ribbon are placed at the back, so that they touched the hair. A hat in fine black lace with brim turned up at on' s side shows a pink pleated velvet bandeau, fastened in place by a handsome paste buckle. It is trimmed with black catrieh plumes and diamond ornar-

For afternoon and visiting toilette' there are numerous dainty fabrics in the way of grenadine, crepon, silk, muslin, and thin hopsack. These are fashioned in many pretty fussy styles—the skirts full and trimmed with frills, edged ribbon. Draped skirts have not LS yet found much favour. A simple style has the skirt raised on one or both sides to show a few inches of the underskirt, which in this way need only be faced with material of a contrast to that used for the dress. Some of the trimming should be repeated on the bodice. Bodices of a contrasting colour to the skirt will be greatly favoured, thus giving many opportunities if wearing our skirta the bodices of whioh are past renovation. Very striking costumes in tailor style, showing the open coat and vest, are made in black mak", with a Louise Seize waistcoat of pale. coloured brocaded satin, with steel or filigree metal buttons. These vests are very characteristic, and have wide pointed revere, which turn oat over the

dark coat. Ths baggue is deep, but cut in one with the vest, and is open from the waist-line, and has little peckets on either side. A costume ot this type needs a j4.bot of soft lace and a jewelled brooch to carry out the style. A capital dress is made of black mob 6, with the front of the skirt trimmed with a panel of black satin covered with butter-co!oured lace in three points, while the high bodice boasts a !rout to match. Yours lovingly,

-JENNIE.

Would that there were more people in the world Ilk e 'Miss can Ingelow," says the " Princess." This well- known poet and novelist makes a point of giving dinners ' three times a week to twelve poor patients freshly dis- charged from the London hospitals. Miss Geselchap is not the least able of the pianists visiting London. The young lady is a native of Java, but received her musical training in Germany. She has been exceedingly successful in the United States, and having been appointed a professor at Boston is compe:led to take up her duties shortly. Mrs. Kruger, wife of the President of the South Afri- can Republic, lately experienced her first railway jour- ney, says the " Colonies and India," which she states was most enjoyable. and was greatly astonished at the smoothness with which the presidential saloon travelled. When asked if she was not afraid of an accident, the old lady said with characteristic faith that the " Al- mighty watched over his people in a train exactly as He would in bullock-wagon, so why should there be any fear ?"

It has been recorded that Miss Kate Field, the well- known American journalist and editor, doesn't like Mr. Willard. The reason given is this: It appears that Miss Field made allusion to Mr. Willard in a public speech, when she pronounced his name with the accent on the first syllable. Mr. Willard immediately wrote a note to the bright editor ef Kate Field's " Washin2ton." telling her that the b.atne should be pronounced wit!: the accent on the last syllable. Just why Miss Field should dislike Mr. Willard for this little attention to her mistake is not clear, but that is the story. -

Ilona Eibenschutz, the young Hungarian pianist —who recently gave a recital at Marlborough House, where she had the honour of playing a pianoforte duet with the Princess of Wales—was born at Buda Pesth in, 1872, says " Black and White." She made her first appearance in public in Vienna at five years of age, and afterwards studied for same seven years at the Conser- vatoire in the Austrian 'capital, performing from time to time in Germany, Denmark, Sweden, Norway,Russia, and other count ri es. Mdlle. Eibenschutz made hArr debut in London at the Monday Popular Concerts in January, 1891, at the age frf eighteen, and has since then been I heard at the Cry stal Palace, the London Symphony,and other well-known concerts.

Preserved Rhubarb.

Cut young and tender rhubarb into pieces about two inches long ;if young the skin is not peeled. Put the pieces into a deep dish, and sprinkle over them three- Quarters of a pound to one pound of rhubarb, one pound of fine moist sugar ; leave it for 12 hours, when a large amount of juice will be extracted. Pour all into a pre- serving pan, and let it simmer for about an hour, when the rhubarb will be unbroken ; put in candid peel or ground ginger.

THE DOUBLE LIFE;

OR, THE HAMFION MYSTERY.

By the Author of " DARKWOOD."

CHAPTER Xvlr., THE MEETING. With wild surprise, eis if to marble struck devoid of sense, A stupid moment motionless she stood. THOMSON.

Girâlda was filled with an innocent delight at the I rospect of so soon beholding her mother, as she fully believed she should behold her, upon the boards of the theatre. She was filled with loving impatience to introduce to her her new protector, whose brilliant promises had filled her own heart with gladness. She longed to see her playing a part to an enthusiastic audience. She exulted al- ready in the thought of her mother's professional triumphs. "Dear, dearest mamma!" she thought, as she attired herself in her own room for the evening. "How surprised she will be to see me! How delighted she will be when I tell her all his lord- ship has promised me! I am almost too happy! And then to nsake my joy quite complete, I am to sec this splendidly beautiful Lady Beatrice Hamp- ton! How much I have beard of her lately. I read in the paper of her beauty, her coldness, her admirers, and her jewels. I heard at Trevelyan Park that she had been the betrothed wife of that poor Mr. Geoffrey Trevelyan. And now she is en- gaged to Mr. Geoffrey's enemy, that unpleasant- looking Lord Adlowe. I wonder if she is as heart- less as people say? I know one thing—Lord Tre- velyan would never speak wtth such enthusiasm of the beauty of the Lady Beatrice if he had seen my mother. Ah ! who is so beautiful as she?" • Her lovely young face glowed with unselfish ad- miration as she recalled the face that represented in her eyes the highest type of beauty and loveli- ness.

Her toilet progressed swiftly, although her little maid was a hindrance rather than a nelp. It re- quired little time or thought to select from a ward- robe so limited as hers. Her black silk dress, with its abundance of pretty little ruffles, was don- ned, and her tiny clustering tresses clung in dusky ripples close to her small and classic heo.d. A narrow white ribbon was wreathed amid her curls, and gathered in an elaborate little bow above her forehead.

Thus attired, she descended to the little private parlour, where Lord Trevelya'n, his lame foot on the polished fender, awaited her coming. He turned and looked at her, at first with an ex- pression of surprise at the severe simplicity of her costume, and then his grand and massive features beamed with an approving and admiring smile. Grim as his nature had grown to be, he could not look coldly upon the vision of fairy loveliness before him. _ _

Blushing and glowing, with her wide, sunny eyes uplifted, that he might look full into their splendours, which fairly rivalled those of purple, tropic skies, a smile on her scarlet lips, a world of innocent witchery in her glances—what wonder was it that the old marquis's face softened into an expression of actual tenderness, and that he looked upon the graceful little creature with a pride and yearning fondness that almost startled himself. "How strangely I feel drawn toward you, my child," he said, rising as she came nearer, and lay-. Lag his large white hand upon her little dark head. " I feel towards you as I once thought I should feel towards Geoffrey's children. You look strangely familiar to me to-night, Giralda, in your bright gayety. As the villagers said, you bear a strong resemblance to the Trevelyans. Can it be that we are of the same blood, and remotely connected ? Your mother is an Englishwoman. I !lust talk with her on the subject. It would give me great pleasure to find that it is instinct which so strangely draws me towards you, my child." "I wish it might prove so," replied Giralda, istrnestly, showing, unconsciously to herself, with what filial warmth of feeling she had begun to regard the lonely and eccentric old nobleman. Lord Trevelyan smiled and crossed the floor to a sofa, upon which lay a little parcel, which he hastened to unfold. "I thought, from the fact that your luggage consisted only of a travelling bag, that your wardrobe, could not be very complete," he said, kindly. "If lam to be your grandfather, Giralda, I must have a grandfather's privileges. This will brighten up your Quaker toilet to- night." As he concluded he flung over her shoulders an exquisite opera cloak of dainty white cashmere, enriched with the finest French embroidery. A hood was attached to the garment, completing its useful qualities. "I went out and purchased the wrap while you were dressing," remarked hie lordship. "This will give effect to your costume." He drew from his pocket a tiny velvet casket, which, on being opened, displayed a brooch of costly pearls. "I noticed that you did not wear ear-rings," he said, placing his gift in her hands. "Put it on, Giralda. The Lady Beatrice Hampton will weirder who you are, and I should like you to make a good impression on her. Of course you cannot rival her ladysh p in magnificence ot jew- els or attire, but your youth and sparkling love- liness will make you a formidable rival to her, if I mistake not." • Giralda murmured her thanks for the gifts, and hastened to fasten her narrow lace collar with the shimmering brooch, whose milky whittneas and rare lustre told to her experienced eyes its great value. • She had scarcely accomplished the task when Wigby announced that the carriage was in wait- int iving his arm to his young charge with a courtly air, Lord Trevelyan led her down to the vehicle, masted her to enter it, and they drove rapidly towards the theatre. "It was a providence that sent yor to me, Gir- sada " said the marquis, in a tone of deep content, as he looked at the lovely little figure sitting op- posite to him. "My life has gained what it needed —an object of tender interest. I love you already, and I know that, grins and cynical as I am, and hard and miserly as I am reported, you love me in return. There is no guile in you, my child. People have said that my soul was but a dry end barren desert, but you have found an overflowing spring in it. A week ago I would not have be- lieved myself capable of my present feelings. I have discovered that there is no one with a heart utterly dead to human passions and hopes. I am Impatient to see your parents, that I may claim you as my granddaughter." "I hope we shall see mamma to-night said Gir- alda, beginning to tremble with anticipation. "Oh, my lord, she is as beautiful as a dream, as loving and tender as an angel lam impatient for you to see her." This impatience began to mingle with a strange foreboding as the carriage halted before the box entrance of the theatre. Looking down on her face as she clung to his arm, after alighting from the vehicle, the marquis noticed how suddenly pale she had grown. "Courage, my child," he said, in an undertone, fancying that he could hear the violent beating of her heart. "Your mamma will not be displeased at your coming. My age renders me a safe pro- tector for you. Think of the joyous surprise that awaits her." He conducted his young charge to their pi irate box, and seated her so that she could command an excellent view of the entire house, as well as the stage. fie drew his own seat close beside her, and began to indicate to her the various sights worthy her observation. The curtain had not yet been drawn up, but the house was filled. It was a marvellous sight that spread before the country-bred eyes of Giralda. She had never before been in any place of public assemblage excepting a church. She was surprised, almost bewildered, at the faces and sights that met her gaze, but her surprise was not apparent in her manner. "That box opposite," said Lord Trevelyan, "is the royal box. Perhaps you will have the pleasure of seeing some of the younger members of the royal family here to-night. The one immediately oppo- site us is the Hampton box. As you see, both boxes are empty. The boxes on this side are all filled. The parquet is full of the aristocracy. That is the pit behind the parquet, and the soberly dressed people Av It o occupy it are what is called the bone' and.' sinew 'of the country. They are the workers, who have come here for relaxation. The occupants of the parquet are the idlers, for the most part, who come here for display." • Giralda looked earnestly at the two classes indi- cated. The parquet chairs were filled with gayly dressed people, upon whose persons jewels flashed and elegant costumes were displayed. Yet even her young eyes detected among the many faces in the aristocratic rows some that exhibited marked ability and character.

" lhose are tile faces of men anti women wilco have risen from the ranit," said the marquis. "There is nothing like work to dignify a man. That noble-looking man in the first row, with the keen eyes, is a successful and famous engineer. That man in the corner chair is a celebrated pub- lisher. Behind him sits an author. These ener-

getic, successful men who have made their fortunes with their own hands, who have carved out their present positions by the exercise of their own abilities—these are the true aristocracy of the earth, Giralda. A man whether rich or poor, if without object or aim in life, is not worthy of respect. You will see many faces in all parts of the house, my child, worth studying. There's a. young fellow near the pit door. See what a study look he has. He is a worker and will boa sue- ___

cessful one some day. Contrast him with that dandy fop over there in the dress-circle!" Before Girald,a could reply the curtain rose and the first act began. The maiden looked at the stage in sudden diaap-

pointment. She is not there, my lord 1" she whispered. .91 • fhe marquis glanced at his play-bill. " Then we have mistaken the theatre," he an-

swered. ‘. The leading fenale characters appear in the first scene in this play. Never mind, my child. We will discover your mother to-morrow, if we have to call at every theatre in town. Don't forget the pleasure you have still in store—that of seeing the Lady Beatrice Hampton I" Giralda resolutely repressed her grief, and de- voted her attention to the stage, unconscious of the many eyes and glasses directed towards her. But her protector was not so unconscious of the admiration she excited. His heart beamed with an exultant pride, as he regarded now and then her spirituelle face, so exquisitely beautiful in its fea- tures and contour, and with the wild-rose bloom glowing and fading in her cheeks, and her great eyes glowing with a happy excitement. They were a handsome couple—she so fair and dainty and pure, he so grand and colossal, with his grizzled hair flung back from his leonine browa,,i and his firm, wide mouth wreathed in a smile made up half of cynicism and half of pleasure. There were many present who knew him, but not one among all his acquaintances there had ever seen a look of such kindliness upon his face since the night that had wrecked his faith in his former idol, Geoffrey Trevelyan. At the end of the first act there was a stir aad flutter in the Hampton box. "The Lady Beatrice is coming," said the mar- quis, glancing in that direction. "Look, Giralda I You will now see the greatest beauty in England— the most heartless woman in the world! Geoffrey Trevelyan worshipped her. Lord Adlowe has placed himself under her Juggernaut car. She has more admirers than any ten women in Great Britain."

Giralda followed his lordship's glances in eager, girlish curiosity. She saw entering the box, or rather advancing to its front, an elderly gentleman of stately pree- ence, with courtly features, to which clung aa expression of melancholy. "Lord Hampton—the father of the Lady Beat- rice," whispered the marquis. Lord Hampton took his seat in a corner of the box, in the shadow of the lace curtains. The next moment Lord Adlowe appeared in view. He was in full evening dress' and was as full of airs and graces as a courtier. He placed a seat for the Lady Beatrice in the front of the box, and took his place beside her. Giralda saw at first only a glorious vision of a stately woman' robed in maize-colored moire—a Cleop*tra-like being with statue-like mien and queenly presence' about whom floated folds and flounces of misty lace, and upon whose neck and arms shone and gleamed and sparkled a perfect forest of brilliant gems. "Is she not magnificent! Is she not a perfect empress ?" whispered Lord Trevelyan. Did you ever see a woman so beautiful?" A mist seemed to gather before Giralda's eyes. When she had brushed it away, and turned her gaze towards the Hampton box, the earl's daughter had settled in her seat and was trifling with her lorgnette while she listened to some whispering words of Lord Adlowe, who was apparently direct- ing her attention to the proteg6e of his uncle. The Lady Beatrice smiled, lifted her opera-glass, and swept a glance at the opposite box. It was a wild and startled face her gaze enconnte.red—a young and lovely face that was blanchod to the line of death—a face full of aa awful terror and anguish! The eyes of the two met fully. They recognised each other in that one wild glance. Giralda uttered no sound—not a word. Only her wild look deepened, and her pallor, if that were possible, increased. With the impulse of a wounded deer, to conoeal herself she drew back behind the curtains.

At the same moment the lorgnette of the Lady Beatrice dropped from her hands, and she sank back fainting into unconsciousness. The shock of meeting her missing daughter there—under the protection of her husband's deadliest enemy—overcame even her self-possession and control. Lord Adlowe alone had marked the cause of her ladyship's sudden illness. • He also had seen that wild look of recognition that had been interchanged between the Lady Beatrice and the young secretary of Lord Tre- velyan.

He stored away the remembrance in his scheming brain for future use. The Lady Beatrice had scarcely fainted when, with real concern, he hastened to assist in her recovery. "It's the heat," said the earl, assisting him to chafe her ladyship a hands. " 1 ne% et' new Beatrice to faint before. She cannot be well—" " The heat was too much for her," declared Ad- lowe, detaching a tiny gold vinaigrett e from the lady's chatelaine, and holding it to her nostrils. The house is crowded. I feel faint in 3 Lord Hampton drew his daughter's chair to the rear of the box Out of the gaze of the multitude, anti devoted himself with agonised tenderness to her restoration. In a few moments his exertions were rewarded WI he desired. His Staughter opened her eyes with a wild and frightened expression. " Do you feel better now, Beatrice r asked her father, tenderly. . " Yes. It was only a sudden faintness," an- swered her ladyship, feebly, drawing her hanc-from Adlowe's clasp. " I have not been well for a week, ' as you know, papa. I am sorry to spoil your pleasure, but I really must go home." " Take my arm, Lady Beatrice," said Lord Ad- lowe, offering the member. " We shall find a cals near the entrance, SO there will be no delay." The earl's daughter arose, and laid her hand lightly on his lordship's arm. • "Trust in me," whispered Adlowe, bending his head down to her. "I would not betray you for the worht I 'lnc cut- d LilA• bli.kft;C: Lt:e eih Übe 4JI • your fainting." The Lady Beatrice glanced into Adlowe's mean- ing face.' She shuildere,i involuntarily, awl diew her wrappings around her as if she felt suddenly cold. "Let us go ntonce, Beatrice," said her father. "I am anxious atout yen." They hastened their departure from the theatre. " Quite a little scene," observed Lord Trevel- yan, when the trio had disappeared from his view, he having watched them so eloely as to forget until now the very exiettnee of his young companion. " What could have made her lady- ship faint away? I didn't suppose it was possible for her to faint—she seems so above all weak- ness af every sort. Well, Girahla, what did you think of her ? Were you not astouished ?" He turned his glance on his young prottg4e. It was a very white little face that looked back at him—a wan little face, without bloom or bright- ness. "Oh, please, my lord," whispered the young girl in agitation, let Ls go away from here ! I don't want to see the play Please take me back to the hotel ! " His lordship looked surprised. "Are you so disappointed at not seeing your mother ?" he asked. " Or are you ill in sympathy with the Lady Beatrice? Her fainting has fright- ened you ?" Giralda did not answer, save by her pleading eyes. • "We will go," said Lord Trevelyan, "If you were not a little country-bred girl, yon would not be so easily alarmed. Come, my child." He gave her his arm, and escorted her from the theatre. A cab was found at the entrance, and the two drove directly to their hotel. • "You are tired," said the marquis, as they ascended the stairs to their sitting-room. " should have known better than to take you to a theatre this evening, after our journey to London. You had better go to bed at once, Giralda. I want to see a bright and blooming lace in the morning. Why. ill take von as you are to vuur msnana,

she will reiuse my request_at.the outset, ana believe me an old ogre a thousand times worse than f am described." _ _

He drew her into the sitting-room and pressed upon her cold forehead a fatherly kiss. He thought at first th -•,t she shrank from his caress—his touch —but he dkuissed the idea when, after a moment of indecision, she flung her a7ms around his neck and kissed him with -thildish fervour. _ _ _

"I will go up to my room now, my lord," she said, in a low, tremulous voice. "I will try and be myself to-morrow." "You love me, d(pn't you, my child ? " asked his lordship, holding her littlu fluttering hand. "You do begin to return the alLction I have already bestowed upon you ? " "Yes, yes, my lord. Ido love you. Good night" She Blipped frpm his embrace and went up Imp her room. _ _

A few minutes later she stole down again, her white cloak changed for a thicker wrapping, her face hidden by a veil, and descended into the street.

MR. SPURGEON'S GARDENER.

Speaking of the great preacher,a former gardener of his says : •` Mr. Spurgeon was one of the most genial and ' pleasant of mem. Although known all over the world,he never pat on any of the airs of a great man. He was on a level with the people in sympathy and feeling, which helped to give him his vast influen cc for good. He would often come into the garden and talk to me about the flowers, especially asking if I understood their Latin names. He was an excellent Lltin scholar, and one had to call up all his wits to discuss the subject with him.' Branching off on a topic more personal to himself, the gardener (Mr. James Fuller) continuud : "My calling ought to he, and is, a healthy one, but no man can ever tell when and why illness may come. Up to February, 1888, I was strong and well. Then I found my appetite failing Ills, and my tongue looked like a piece of brown , leather. What little I ate gave ma pain in my chest and eidea,and I had also diarrtucea with severe griping pains. My hands and feet were cold and clammy ; indeed I felt cold all over my body. " About a week later I had an attack of rheumatic fev(-r. At first this took me in the hips and shoulders and then spread to every joint, and I went to bed and sent for a doctor. For weeks I was in great agony and helpless as a baby, not able to move hand or foot. Out- ward applications gave but little relief. I was harrassed by a dry hacking cough, with much retching and strain- ing to raise the phlegm. I was in this state for over two months, during which time four doctors prescribed for me, but nothing did me any real good. _ _

'One of these physicians, after sounding me, said " Fuller, your ailment is the first stage of consumption."

He was mistaken, however. "Some time later I went to the Great Homer Street Hospital, where I received every attention, and several th,ctors took great interest in my case. My stomach was so weak that for some time I was put on starvation diet. After a while I left the hospital, the doctors say- ing they had done all they could for me. In a weak, emaciated condition I returned home. After a few days' rest, I went to Freckniliam, near Newmarket, to see if my native air would help me. Arriving there I became so had I could barely walk, my cough and the spitting all the time getting worse and my breathing short and hurried.

"Despondent and feeble enough by now, I went:to Bury St. Edmunds, and consulted a celebrated specia- list, who gave me medicines, but held out no hope of my recovery. _ _ _

" Time ran along until June, 1888, when I heard some of my Freekenharn friends speak so highly of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup that I determined to try it. In four days I found relief. My appetite returned,

food agreed with me, and I gained strength fast. My cough and the other troublesome.symptoms soon left me. t 'ken the syrup for a few weeks, I was able to return to Norwood and go to work. One old friend who called to see me was astonished, and said to my wife, Well, well ; I should not have been surprised to hear that your husband was dead.' Thirteen years ago came to Norwood as gardener to the late Rev. C. H.; Spurgeon, and lived at the lodge, Westwood House. I tell everyone that I am indebted for my life to Seigel's

Syrup." Mr. Fuller's present address is 42,Queen's road, Crown Hill, Upper Norwood, near London. On this short, clear statement only little comment is called for. In the first place it must be distinctly wider-

stood that Mr. Fuller did not have consumption or any

other organic lung affection whatever. The symptoms which looked that Way arose from the stomach, and from nothing else. The cough was as• stomach cough" and the short breath was asthma,resulting from a poison in the blood acting upon the nerves which control the lungs. When Mother's Seigel's Curative Syrup had set the digesting machinery in order, and expelled the im- purities from the blood, the alarming portents disap- peared as a matter of course. The point is this : give yourself up to die of consumption until you abso- lutely know you have consumption. The chances are a hundred to one that you have what Mr. Fuller had,indi- geEtion and dyspepsia, which can imitate and counter- feit almost any disease under the sun. •

.Fly Cages.

Take a square of tissue paper (about 12 inches), fold It across from point to point, to form a triangle, fold again the same way three times more, which will form a V. Then commence to cut across within a± of an, inch of the other side, turn and cut the same may, leaving about a±of an inch between each cutting, turn al-out till the top is reached, cut off the ends, open carefully. Shake out very gently. take up opposite loops across, and tie with a bit of thread. making a loop to hang by. They look very nice made aotible of different colours, putting one inside the other when finished.

Artificial Silk. A company has recehtly been formed in Bradford for the purpose of manufacturing artificial silk, according to the methods of Dr. F. Lehner. The new material ii anufa,:ttured from an emulsion si:nilar to collodion, made from vegetable pulp. The artificial silk takes 11 shades of dyes exactly the same as pure silk, and al- -though not quite so strong as the best pure silk (rela- tively 4to 6). it is strong enough for the process of weaving. The brilliancy And adaptability for all pur- poses where silk is required and its cheapness (about half the price of the genuine article) will, it is thought, bring the new invention to the front. Bradford will be the centre of the new manufacture.

Diphtheria. Is it contagious? Undoubtedly. We know that it is contagious, although we are not acquainted with the exact mode in which the contagion operates. The infec- tious matter is not capable of any wide diffusion through the air, hut clings in the-most persistent manner to par- ticular places, houses, and even rooms. There is very little doubt that it can be inoculated. M. Valleix, a well known and esteemed French surgeon and writer, was in attendance upon a little girl suffering from diphtheritic sore throat. Under energetic treatment she recovered. One day, however, while M. Valleix was inspecting her throat he received into his mouth a small quantity of saliva driven out of that of the patient in the act of ' coughing. Next day a little exudation appeared on one of his tonsils. The other tonsil and the adjacent parts became speedily covered with false membrane,a, profuse discharge took place from the nostrils, delirium super- vened, and in forty-eight hours M. Valleix was dead. In ancther case of diphtheria the medical man in attend- ance found it necessary to open the windpipe t) relieve the breathing. There was some obstruction from the accumulation of blood, when the operator, to save the patient's life, applied his mouth- to the wound in the neck, and sucked the fluid out. In forty-eight hours he died from symptoms identical with those from which M. Valleix suffered. There is very little doubt that diph- theria is often produced by drinking infected milk. The medical officer of the Local Government Board in his report on diphtheria for the year 1888-9 says :—" Dis- semination of diphtheria by milk is now in this country so generally accepted and has, moveover, unhappily been so often demonstrated that the fact of such dis- semination of the disease netds not to be insisted on."— " The Family Physician."

THE PLNTTS MILLIONS.

RIVAL CLAIMANTS AT BRADFORD.

George Platts, an employee of the Midland Railway Company at Derby, some time ago saw a paragraph in the Derby Express" which ran :—" Windfall to a Birkenhead Fatnilv.—A Bitkenhead family of the name of Platt: has just fallen into an enermous fortune of £4,000.000, left by a relative who recently died in America." He and his hiends made inquiries into the windfall, and found that they were the family of Plat's immediately connected with the wealthy relative In America. They are endeavaatring to establish their claim to the enormous fortune. George Platts and his rela- tives, it is understood, base their claim to the fortune on the ground that their grandfather, who died at Birken- head, and whose son died at SaratnEta, Springs, New York, was brother to the four times millionaire. Just in the same way that Georg'? Platts became aware of his relative having died wealthy, an old Derby family of Platts, now residing in Bradford, saw in the " Bradford Argus" a paragraph referring to the Derby windfall. They met and decided to investigate whether the mil- lionaire referred to was their grandfather, who early in life, they understood, left Derby for the States, where he amassed considerable wealth as a builder in the pahniesr days, which they always understood had been much in- creased by large contracting works in -Australia, and ' again subsequently in America. Their rich relative, who at one time was a mason, had been the cause of much speculation, and when they heard of the expected D-rby windfall they were cc the gni rive. The leader. Henry Platts, now a draper, visited Derby, and ',brained all certificates going to show that their father and mother were born at Brampton. and christened at the Old Crooked Spire, where the grandfather of the Derby claimant, George, was also. Now the Bradford Platts come forward and contend that they are able to prove that they are the direct descendants of the millionaire, and that they will establish their claim to the millions without loss of time in unrefutable manner. They are now taking the necessary steps, and their solictor.throngh the American Consul in Bradford, is lodging the claim. The solicitor and the family, the latter all of the working lass, are maintaining a quiet confidence in their ability .o prove their rights.

MRS. GLA.DSTONE AS A PIJILAN-

TH ROPIST.

Mrs. Gladstone's well-known Convalescent Home at Woodford had its origin ni.4h thirty years ago. At that time such institutions were almost unknown. One at Walton-on-Thames, with branches for children at Mit- cham and Hendon, had been established in 1840 ; but what were these for the needs cf the London hospitals ? Mrs. Glad,tone, therefore, assisted by Miss Catherine Marsh, proceeded to found a new establishment. Its origin was, as the lade herself has declared, in the cholera epidemic of HIM. Mrs. Gladstone was then viiming the wards of the Lond.n Hospital.and there met Dr. (afterwards Sir Andrew) Clark as the senior physi- cian of the hospital. She was, says the "Quiver," much struck by his great ability and his great tenderness for the sick ; and when she determined to found the Wood- bird Home. Dr. Clark rendered her invaluable assistance. He gave much time and attention to the scheme, and prepared the rules for its management, which have been in force ever since. The meeting with Dr. Clark at the London Hospital ripened into a great friendship,land the doct,r became the highly valued triend and physician of Mr.and Mrs. Gladstone. That lady's Convalescent Home is, and has been for the service of the p,or, more parti- cularly of East London ; and convalescents from their own home:, as well as from hospialt, are received free ,1 cost. It is, of course, quite unsectarian ; and there are, Moreover, no privileged tickets. The cases of ad misition are determined each on its own merits. The expenditure amounts to about £1.200 to £1,300 per annum, while the patients admitted number nearly a thousand in the year. Since then the numbers of Con- valescent Homes have largely increased. Their benefi- cence must tie very great. Not only do tin y relieve tbe pressnre on the hospitals themselves, but they aff rd means whereby, for a small fee, or gratuitously,the vic- tory over disease may be decisively won ; and as Mrs. filadstom's Home was one of the earliest,that lady may be regarded as one of the pioneers in . the movement of founding these important institutions.

AN OLD SPORTING GAME.

At St. Helens Police Court, on Monday, before Mr.W. J. Thompson, Mr. C. Sharpies, and Mr. B. B. Glover, a rough-looking middle-aged man named James Egan, of Newcastle-on-Tyne,. WaA char4ed uneer the Vagrancy Act, with playing a game of chance called " Aunt Sally." Police-constable Patterson said that on Saturday after- noon hp saw Egan on the fair ground at Traverse-st. Fie waa playing a game of chance, and making bets of. 2 and 3to 1. witness explained to the magistrates how the game was played. Egan got a small can, in which he placed some clay. A small piece of stick was put upright, in the clay, and on the top there was a button. The person who played it took a piece of wire,and stand- ing tour feet away tried to knock the button ofr so as to make it fall on the opposite side. saIMIMmiEIIEM

INDUSTRIES THAT ROB THE DOCTOR.

WORKERS WHO ARE RARELY ILL.

If special diseases are identified with many industries, there is a set-cif to this in the fact that those who follow particular occupations are thereby rendered disease- proof. Awt 11-known writer has often told us that dur- ing a certain terrible epidemic of cholera in London not one tobacconist was numbered among the victims. A parallsl faet was given to the writer the other day by a tobaccaii a luf icturer. "It's a very remarkable thing," said he, "out none of my hands have been down with the influenza. It has raged all round us five or six times, but we have every one escaped unscathed." Equally fortunate, probably, have been salt and chemical workers. The former class seem to become so impreg- nated with the saline vapour which floats around them that they can defy disease. They cannot, apparently, catch cold even. At Droitwich, in the depth of winter, the writer has seen scantily-clad hands come straight from the pan and, without putting on scarf or coat, sit down in the open air for some time. To most people • this infringement of the elementary laws of hygiene would be punished by this ; but they take no harm what- ever. The Cheahire salt-workers, who are likewise pickled, so to speak, are also rarely ill, many of them lying to a good round age. Chemical labourers are ac- cording to a writer in " Cassell's Saturday Journal," never attacked by any infectious disease, nor are they often troubled with those little ailments which prevent • ,so many of us from realising Pepe's ideal of "all the joys of sense'—" health, peace, and competence." Agan, you often see mothers taking their children to • chemical works, in order that the little ones may breathe • the pungent fumes which arise from freshly-drawn salt cake. According to popular belief, those fumes are - " good " for quite a number of infantile disorders. They are disagreeable ersough for anything. In the same way, gas-works are visited by women whose children are , sufferiae. frons whooping-cough, a cure for which is in- haling the smoke that rises from hot tar or pitch. More • unpleasant still is an industrial remedy for rheumatism, of which the writer was told by a sailor. Those who work among guano, when racked with rheumatism, . Plunge the member affected into that product, allowing it to remain covered for some time, and are invariably, relieved and generally cured thereby. Talking of rheu- matism, by the way, in some towns there is a great demand for one of the residual products of resin refining. It is a liquid which apparently contains a large propor- tion of turpentine, and at the works it is given away to • anybody who applies for it, and brings his own bottle. Most remarkable of all, however, is an industry which has a highly beneficial effect on consumption—that of Imp-black making, to wit. The men who feed the fires which consume the waste oils from which this article of commerce is made work in a smoke-laden atmosphere, and hence inhale some of the carbon or lamp-black, . which is said to afford great relief to the lungs. Many consumptives, in fact, apply for work at lamp-black and . ink factories. If a man could only earn his bread at an industry suitable for his physical weak point, his life might often be lengthened materially.

RUItiCORN COUNTY COURT.

TUESDAY.—Before His Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes. - FARMERS' SQUABBLE AT KINGSLEY.—This -was an action brought by John Jones, team owner of Kingsley, . against Edwin Alderman, farmer and coal dealer of the same locality, for £5O damages as compensation for injuries sustained through being, as plaintiff alleged, assaulted by the defendant on the 2nd March.—Mr. W. H. Churton, (Chester) appeared for plaintiff, and Mr. Fowler (from the office of H. Davies and Co., Warring- ton) represented defendant. The plaintiff's case was that the defendant was the tenant of a field at Kingsley, and a man named Oultram, a shoemaker, el Kinsgley, believing that he was entitled to take possession of this field on the Ist of March last, employed the plaintiff to plough it. On the following day Oultram took the plaintiff to the field, and Jones, who had no concern with the differences of Oultram and the defendant, commenced to plough the field as requested. They had ben in the field about ten minutes when the defendant, accompanied by his two sons appeared on the scene with a shovel in his hands, and proceeded to stop Jones from. during his work. Tne plaintiff did not attempt to con- inue after this interference of toe defendant, but. Oultram, requested the defendant to leave the field This Alderman refused to do, and his wife and another person then came up. An altercation ensued, and at the terinin ition of the row the defendant, as he left the field, called out to Jones, " I'll murder you, if it is thirty or fifty years to come." Alderman was in a state of great excitement, and Jones, accompanied by Oultram, pro- cee:fed to the stable, where they were followed by the defendant, his wife, two sons, and three girls, armed with shovels, potato forks, and sticks. They commenced an attack on Oultram and the plaintiff with those weapons. The latter was standing at the stable door, and, seeing Oultram (who is an old man) being mal- treated, asked the defendants and the other assailauts te desist. Upon this Alderman took up a shovel, with which he struck the plaintiff a violent blow on the head, knocking him down, defendant repeating the blow as plaintiff was falling to the ground. Defendant then left, accompanied by the others. As a result of the injuries plaintiff suffered considerably, and was unable to follow his emploTment for six weeks. Summonses were origin- ally issued for criminal proceedings, but were subse- quently withdrawn.—The defendant gave a denial to the statement of the plaintiff and his witnesses, and made a counter-claim for £5O for personal injuries. He alleged that he was roughly assaulted by Oultram and plaintiff. The latter, he said, struck him on the head with a potato fork, inflicting serious wounds, which necessitated medi- cal treat Ment. A large number of witnesses were called on both sides, and the case lasted all day.—His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for £l9 and costs, and judgment for Jones on the counter-claim with costs.

REMARKABLE SCENE AT A LABOUR MEETING.

An extraordinary scene triok pace at Witton Park, Blaelcburn,on Saturday. Nearly 20,000 workers attended a labour demonstration, and there was sp‘aking from four platforms. An attempt was made to from the platform on which Lady Mike was seated, and so great was the uproar that she was guarded to a carriage and driven friLin the park. Several free fleas followed, but the disturbance was ultimately quelled by the police. It is stated that the attack was premeditated, and that it was organised by the Conservative trade unionists, who strongly objected to the resolution against the con- tracting-out clause. On the °tiler hand, thessr men declare that they made no attack on the platform, and had no desire to cause Lady Dilke alarm. They were indignant bsrcause they were not permitted to move an amendment.

AN ADVERTISING DODGE.

You can't always tell by the heading of an article • what it is. It may be an advertisement for Warner's Safe Cure," a safe, sure, and positive cure for diseases of the kidneys and liver, and their developments," and you do not read advertisements if you know it, but you have read this one so far, and ought to finish it, as it Will only take you thirty seconds to read the testimonial of Mr. Jas. S. Joy, 68, Corporation Street, Manchester, dated April 18th, 1894. "About four years ago I had an attack of Influenza, which kept me in bed four weeks. When I got up and about again I was very • nervous; could get no sleep, could not eat, and suffered with pains in the head and face which almost drove me mad. I got worse and worse, week after week. Of cfiurse I had medical advice, and various kinds of medi.H eine, from none of which I received any benefit. When things got to the worst, and everybody, including my- self, had given up all hope, I met a friend whom I had not seen for some time, and seeing the condition I was in, '(I had lost 4 stone in weight), ,he said, Try Warner's Safe Cure.' I laughed at the idea. No quack medicine for me, thank you.' But he insisted, saying, Don't get one bottle and use it, and then say it is no good. Your case is too serious for that. It has taken years to bring You to your present condition, and you won't be cured in a week, but if you will get bottle after bottle for a month, and take same as per instructions, and you do not find yourself well on the road to recovery at the end of that time,! will pay for all you have taken, and stand a guinea silk hat into the bargain.' I yielded and got a large bottle, and religiously took eight doses a day. When that was done I got another, and followed suit. At the end of two weeks I felt no better, though every one • else congratulated me upon the improvement that had taken place. This encouraged me to go on, which I did ' for about four months. At the end of that time I was as well as ever I had been in my life, gaining weight all the time. Since then I had never hal a day's illness, but lam never without a small bottle of Warner's Safe Core in the house. If! feel a little off colour in the morning, I take a dose before breakfast." lam afraid the above is rather a rambling statement, but it is a statement of facts (stubborn things those facts).

A WARRINGTON JOURNALIST'S EXPERIENCES IN AMERICA.

SOME REMINISCENCES.

BY LUXE BLUNT.

It is about live years ago. that Mr. James Bate, formerly assistant librarian at the IVarrington Library, sailed away to America in quest of Dame Fortune's favours. He arrived over here last week, and seems to have secured a fair share of the bounte- ous one's dispensations. He was on his way to Ant- werp, to "write up" the exhibition there, as corres- pondent for the Chicargo "Herald," and was mak- ing the journey in company with a Mr. N. O'Brien, fiNe w York. I waylaid him the other afternoon, intending to put him through an operation, which ke as an Amer ican journalist ef ten subjects other People to, viz, the process of mental dissection known as interviewing. I found that America had not perverted his genial disposition, though it had changed him into the quick decisive sort of man that Yankee journalists are made of. Of the five years he has been abroad he spent four on the staff of the Chicargo " Herald," and one on the staff of the New York" World." American reporters differ from those in England, in that they combine with the " profession of letters," the characteristics of public prosezutors and detectives. So that I was not wrong in surmising that -Mr.l3ate's career across the Atlantic must have been varied and exciting. We fell to talking about his professional experi- ences.

One night while engaged on the" World," he met his city editor, who informed him that he had re- ceived intimations of a shameful state of things Which were alleged to be existing on the quarantine island, in New York harbour. There was a dreadful epidemic of typhus in the city at the time, which caused considerable alarm.

"1 am going to look the matter up, and I want a Volunteer to go with me," said the city editor. "My services are at your dispasal, sir," replied the War- ringtonian ; and after making a few arrangements the couple repaired to a wharf on the East river, and it was now they met with the first check in their dangerous exploit. Anyone approaching the quarantine island is liable to a heavy penalty, and consequently they were unable to ge,.t a boatman to ferry them across. They tried to bribe the offi- cial ferryman; but it was of no avail. Despair mined them as a companion. But he evidently actel as a stimulus, for by a final ruse they -man- aged to induce a boatman to adjourn to an adja- cent saloon to imbibe " cocktails," to the immortal glory of their souls. and to the continuance of their health ; the latter being a sensible toast consider- ing their expediraou. While lie was absent they leaped into the boat, cut the painter by which it Was moored,and rowed away to the island of disease. It was midnight when they arrived, and as dark as It well could be. The victimsof the various diseases Where confined in different pavilions, and towards the first of these they began to grope their way. Presently they stumbled against a tent. After some hesitation they stealthily peered between the canvass, and a horrid, fearsome sight, which set the cold sweat running down their faces, presented itself. Lying on his back, partly covered with a blanket, was the hideous form of a leper Chinaman; a man whose terrible disease compelled him to live alone in this solitary place, and debarred him for ever of having intercourse with a fellow being. They hurried from this ghastly spectacle as rapidly Its the intense darkness would permit; and soon they came upon the first pavilion, into which they entered. But here there awaited their a sight ten- fold more horrible than that of the poor leper. It Was the ward in which the delirious patients were confined. On 18 beds there were strapped down 18 Poor, raving victims; some with the vigour of mad- ness were struggling to free their wasted limbs of their bonds; some were shrieking hideously ana blaspheming wildly, and some with the anguish of dying men, were calling for water, while the blood Oozed from theiemouths and their eyes started from !heir heads withltter Y. And tilers was no one about to minister to these dying creatures' wants • • • . The next pavilion they came to was oc- Cunied by a number of small-pox patients,who,upon the entrance of the clandestine visitors, clied and einnlered, and begged for water. There had been to attendant near them for hours. The two spent sometime in fetching and administering water, and "'long those to whom they gave to drink, they dis- covered a journalist, known to them both : a man ;who had been stricken down while investigat- ing the spread of the disease in the city ; and, as it is proved, was doomed to die within a few aours. For another hour Mr. Bate and his com- Panion continued their investigations, and dis- lovered Only one attendant on duty in the whole Island to watch over 300 sufferers from smallpox I•knd typhus ! As they were preparing to leave the I,!latid they came across a weird sight. Aided by Lne dim light of a couple of lanterns, were three ta, en busily though ,ilently engaged ia interring tnSee dead patients. The men proved to be an at- tendant a ffi convalescent patient, and an ocial. s tnrtlY afterwards the two pressmen pushed off in Wen' small boat and rowed into the darkness. A heavy fog', however, soon enveloped them, anti they were e3mpelled to row without a knowledge of 'whether they were going in the direction of the sea ?? the shore. But it proved they were rowing sea- wards, for an hour afterwards they collided with a vessel that was making its way into harbour, and were only providentially saved from drowning by ~_e • friendly hands that hauled them on board. The ed publicationof.the revolting facts which they had obtain Cleated a great sensation. A public lificluiry by the city authorities was made. at which S. Bate and his editor gave evidence, with the re- li !. It that the medical officer of the island was dis- charged, and a new staff of attendants engaged. An Ltenipt, was made by the other papers to have the '-w.O journalists arrested anti quarantined ; but the agitation came to nothing. As a reward for his the Mr. Elate was presented with a cheque b he y proprietors of the " World," which was in- scribed with " Dresented to Mr. Bate for his volun- tzirY services, &e." ------ continued startling experience, whic% I had," Mr. Bate "was in connection with the winding up of some New York building and loan a.ocieties. At the termination of a prolonged harangue he rose to go; but it was not before I had caught sight of that much wished for report, in a drawer which he had opened. Almost in the same sweep of the vision, my eye had rested upon the key of the office door, and this decided ire in taking a prompt, decisive action. As an ordinary act of courtesy I opened the door for the examiner to allow him to make his exit, and at the same time I dropped the key into my pocket. Within an hour I was back at the office, and having got the cora- missionare out of the way under a pretext, I ran up to the examiner's office, and in another ten minutes I was on my way to the " World " offices with the report in my pocket. So serious was it in import, that the publication of the report caused a panic among the shareholders, and the result of a few more investigations I had to make was that two of the direcf,ors were sentenced to penal servitude for embezzlement ; another was mortally wounded in an assault made on him by an enraged share- holder, and three other directors—well-known men —fled the city."

"And how do the duel-loving Americans take this sort of exposure. Do they, to use a phrase which is somewhat slangy, " cotton " to it." "it has become such an important characteristic of the American journals, that the public regard it as the duty of the papers to take up prosecutions and investigations, and consequently the people incriminated do not as a rule feel any particular bitterness against us. Occasionally they do, and then we have to get out of the danger as best we may."

"Did you ever get a challenge?" "Well not exactly," said Mr. Bate smiling. "But I got an intimation in Chicargo once that I should be shot "on sight." It was in consequence of an investigation made against a merchant whom I will designate as Robinson. One day,the body of a very beautiful girl, who was identified as Carry Smith, was found in Michigan lake. Her death was sup- posed to be the result of suicide or murder. The police and ourselves had been making investigations all day, but not an iota of information could be gathered. At about eleven o'clock that night I learned of an hotel keeper who was supposed to come from the same town as Carry Smith. I jumped into a cab and had soon found my man. "Do you know Carry Smith is drowned," I said. "Is she ! By it's that Robinson's work." "And where is Robinson now ?"

"He's living at -street." I pretended that I knew Robinson, and a conver- sation, judiciously directed, revealed to me these facts: Robinson had been the girl's lover at her home in a neighbouring city; she had eloped with him to Chicago, and had been employed as his type- writer; he was now going to be married to a lady of some note, and he had been seen last with the deceased, Carry Smith. This was enough. I rattled away in a four wheeler and arrived opposite the block of buildings in which Robinson lived. It was now turned midnight. I started with the top bell. A night-capped woman thrust her head out of the window. " Does Mr. Robinson live here ?"

"No, he dosn't ! Come waking people up at this hour for !" and then her voice, uttering impreca- tions was lost to me as I went for the next bell. Similar results rollowed until I got to the very last door, when, in answer to an enquiry, someone at a window said Mr. Robinson did live there.

'• I want to see him on the most important busi- ness."

"Now then, what do yolt want to see me about," said a big man, who appeared at the door, a few seconds after. I locked at his hue frame and realied that he could crush the; and I got a glimpse of his face—a long, sallow, repulsive looking face— and I felt he was a man unhampered with scruples. "I am a" Herald " reporter," .1 said. After a mo- ment's hesitation he replied : "Come wit h me then."

I went. He led me by the hand through a pitch dark room, and then into a passage. Suspicions of foul play—naturally to be expected under such cir- cumstances—began to flash through my brain, and the perspiration rolled down my face. Out of the passage we went into another dark room ; then up a stairway, and presently into a lighted bedroom. There did not appear to be another soul in the house and when this man, having got into bed again, asked me my errand, my knees began to smite each other with something like fear. I knew the safety of my lii e.depended upon diplomacy. I suspected him of the murder of Carry Smith, and intended to have confronted him with the accusation. But it was different now. I made a start: " You knew Carry Smith? " _

" Yes, her body was found this morning." " How did you know it was her body ? " " Well--I thought it was."

"She worked for. -you?"

' She was my typewriter.' "You were her lover? "

" Its-a lie." And be raised his body and glared at me. _

--4fsk it a fact you were with her yesterday even-

" What if I was ?1"

" Well—it looks strange."

" What do you suspect. me of " he n,sked with a terrible scowl on his face, and he raised himself into a sitting position. I made as diplomatic an explanation as I could, and then he said :

" If anything of this gets into the papers I shall short, you on sight." " Very well Mr. Robinson, you may hold me re- sponsible."

The feel I had gatherel were published, and the next evening I got word from another journalist named Lahiff, that he had unearthed something about Robinson. He had found a lawyer to whom a nurse had made some depositions about Robinson, showing that he was already a married man, and he was to be married again the next day, at four o'clock. At three o'clock the next day, the lawyer, and the nurse, myself. and Lahiff, rolled away in a carriage to the Church, where the ceremony was to take place. It was our intention to interrupt the ceremony, and object to the marriage. But unfor- tunately Robinson had been beforehand with us. He had been married at two o'clock. He has not been seen in the city since and of course has not yet " shot me on sight."

"In contrast to this I had a funny experience some time afterwards. A paragraph appeared in a pap.,r that a rich farmer's daughter had been lost in tl:e snow at Danbury. As a means of advertising the" herald " I was sent off to Danbury to organize a search party,and write up.the story. Upon alight- ing from the train at my destination,' got into con- versation with an in',elligent sort of man, and pre- sently told him the object of my errand. There appeared to be romantic possibilities in the case. The man said : - -

" You've come from Chicago for that?" " Yes, sir.' " Wall,vou are a gossoort."

" Oh !how !" " That rich farmer's daughter is a crazy nigger servant wench, who got drunk and lost herself. She's in the house across there now." "I went across. And sure enough it was a crazy nigger girl, and when I got into the room there were two other coloured females chafing her limbs and as they worked were crooning: " Hock ob ages clef' fur me." " Of course the " herald " didu't mention any- thing about this story."

A HOUSEKEEPER'S HISTORY.

An interesting and unusual case has been lately made public in the "Kent County Examiner," a great news- paper published at Ashford. The circumstances are reported in the form of an interview with Mrs. Fester an elderly woman, occupying a trusted position as housekeeper in the establishment of a gentleman at Ran- dolphs, Riddenden.

" Last July," said Mrs. Foster to the reporter, "I had been standing in a draught, and cainTht a sharp attack of rheumatism in my left arm, it was so bad that I had great trouble in moving my arm at all, and it gave me dreadful pain. It was soon so bad that I found it impos- sible to dress nisNelf. For months I could not put ray hands far enough behind me to tie my own apron strings ! I suffered a great deal of pain, and tried many advertised remedies, but not one of them was of the slightest use." " Well," said the "Kent Examiner" reporter, "it is this that I come to ask you about. It seemed that you had been cured in some miraculous way." • "Yes, I am certainly cured though medicine was of no use; hut it was by medicine, too, that I was cured, only you see it was not ordinary medicine. I had read something in the newspapers of the new scientific remedy called Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I ob- tained a supply, andln five days, on coming downstairs one morning, found I could move my left arm in a way that had not been possible to me for months; not since the attack first came on in fact."

" Had you to take the long, after that ?" "Oh, no ; the cure was all done by half a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I have had no rheumatism since; and that was about two months ago." Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are an unfailing cure for rheu- matism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus dance, nervous headache, prostration, &c. They give the glow of health,. are a specific for troubles of the fern de sex, and in rrPn eff?ct a radicil cure in CaS9Sariiinz from mental worry, overwork, or excesses of any kind.

They are sold by all Chemists, or may be hai direct from the Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 46, Hol- born Viaduct, London, at 2s. 9,1. a box, or six boxes for 13s. 91. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale reaple are never sold loose, and any dealer who offers substitutes in this or any form is trying to defraud ; the wooden box must be in a pink wraptvir, b )tring-, the fall name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills far Pale People.

WIDNES ATHLETIC CLUB.

The twenty-seventh annual festival of the Widnes Athletic Club Sports was held on the Sports Field, Farnworth on Friday and Saturday. MARRIAGE OF MR. W. P. RYLANDS, AND MISS N. DE ANGELIS.

The marriage of Mr. Wm. Peter Rylands,youngest son of the late Mr. Peter Rylands, M.P., and Miss Nora de Anglis, niece of Mr. W. H. Bleckly of Thelwall Lea, took place the other day at Coniston Parish Church. The weather was all that could be desired, and Coniston Lake and the surrounding hills were looking at their best. The bride was picturesquely attired in a gown of white serge. The skirt was plain, .and the bodice was cut like a short jacket, open to show a vest of white brocaded silk,with revers of the same material, and trimmed with large peatl buttons. She wore a becoming hat with ostrich feathers, and a beautiful necklet pendant and bracelet of pearls, the gifts of the bridegroom. She carried a splendid white bouquet. The bride was attended by two bridesmaids, her cousin, Miss Maud B sekly, and Miss Georgiana Rylands, sister of the bridegroom. The former wore a pretty light grey costume, with large hat and feathers to match, arid the latter Was attired in a white cashmere dress, .trimmed with pale green em- broidery and strait silk of the same shade, and wore a large white hat, with a green silk bow and white ostrich feathers. Miss Bleckly wore a gold padlock bracelet, and Miss G. Rylands, a gold hare breoch. and each carried a beautiful shower bouquet, composed of pink carnations, white loses, and stepanotis, all gifts from the bride- groom. Mr Eiward Williams, of Rochford House, Ten- bury, acted as best man, and Mr. W. H. Bleckly gave the bride away. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. T. Pym Williamson, vicar of Thelwall, assisted by the Rev.C. Chapman, vicar oh Caniston. After the ser- vice, the school children stlod on either side of the churchyard path and se stP.ered fl-iwers before the happy pair as they passed on to the carriage that was awaiting them. The villagers had killed a tine fox that same morning, and poor Reynard's body was hung up, head downwards, to one of the gateposts in the churchyard. This is evidently an old marriage custom in the aeigh- bourhood, and is supposed to bring good luck to the newly-wedded couple. The wedding party ti ten repaired to the Waterhead Hotel for luncheon, and the guests were principally relatives of the bride and bridegroom. Mrs. Peter Rylands WAS present, dressed in a handsome black satin costume trimmed with mauve brocade, and Mrs. Bleckly wore a black grenadine over white silk. Mr. and Mrs. W. P. Rylands, left at three o'clock to drive to Windermere, eis route for Scotland, where the honeymoon will be spent. The bride's travelling cos- tume consisted of a simple jacket and skirt of blue serge, and a black hat tastefully trimmed with roses. The wedding presents, which were on view at Thelwall Lea for seine days previously,were numerous and costly,and testified to the esteem in which Mr. and Mrs. W. I'. Itylands are held by their many friends.

A man named Padabock can tell a story of queer re- verses of fortune. In 1828 he went to the Pacific Coast as a sailor, and there married the daughter of a Mexican rancher, from whom he received the ground that is now occupied as the site of San Francisco. Poverty overtook him, and he obtained admission to the poorhouse in that, city recently. Ile is 91 years of age.

WARRINGTON INFIIIMARY AND DISPENSAIZY.

THE DEFICIT: AN APPEAL FOR HELP.

A specially-convened meeting was held at the Infirmary, Kendrick-st.,ou Friday afternoon,for the purpose of devising the best scheme for raising the deficit of £1,200 which is at present owing on the extension account. Major Picicmere occupied the chair, and there were also present : Messrs. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., J. Fairclough.J.P., T. Birehall, J.P., Rev. H. V. Pigot, Drs. White, Fox, Richmond, and Bowden, Messrs. T. Morris, F.G.S., D. Fanshaw, J. Roberts, Thompson, Dakin (hon. treasurer), J. L. Tunstall (hon. sec.), and C. Lee (assistant secretary).—Mr. Tunstall referred to a letter he had received from Mr. E. J. Guest referring to the Board's decision at its previous meeting in reference to a workman named Pointer. The letter was as follows :—" Oak Mount, Helsby,26th May, 1894. J. L. Tunstall, Esq. • Dear Sir,—l am in receipt of your favour of the 25th inst., and regret the decision of the Board of Management aa I fear it will have a depressing effect on our men who are willing subscribers to thn Infirmary. It is true Pointer is in receipt 'if 21s. a week when in full work but he has been ill himself and his wages do not come in. If such men as he are not to reap any advan- age in times of sickness I fear a good deal of persuasion will be required to prevail upcn them to subscribe to the funds when they are well.—Yours faithfully, E. J. Guest." A long discussion took place as to the advis- ability of adhering to the rules of the institution as to the giving of free medical attendance. The rules as at present, were to the effect, that no person was entitled to free medical advice at the Infirmary, who received more than 153. per week for one person ; 16s. for two persons ; 21s. for three persons, arid so on at the same ratio. As the man in question was a single map, and (earning 21s. per week, the Board decided to adhere to the rules on the proposition of the t_ hairman. Mr. Thompson said he would move at a future meeting that the sum at which a person could obtain free medical attendance. should be raised to 183. per week for one person.—Mr. Dakin read the statrment of accounts of the Infirmary Extension Fund. The expenditure had been £5,479 14s. 7d. ; the subscriptions towards this amount, were £4,131 133. 2d., which with £Bl 53. 7d. bank interest left a balance owing to the bank on that account of £1,266 15s. 10d. 'The Secretary re id a letter from Mr. Parr, in which he offered £lOO towards the liquidation of the debt on condition that the balance was raised, Mrs. Beamont wrote stating that she would be willing to subscribe £lOO, and if necessary a further con- tribution of £lOO. She deprecated the idea cf having a bazaar in raising funds towards the deficit. Mr. Dakin said he had explained the position of affairs to Mr. John Crosfiefd, J.P., and that gentlemen had promised fa dona- tion of £lOO. Mr. J. R. fluckton al•z;) wrote stating that he was willing to subscribe £2O. Major Pickmere thought that the best mode of raising the amount re- quired would be for a deputation to beappoisted to wait personally on local manufacturPrs, and others to a.,k them to subscribe, He felt certain that the money re- quired would be raised. On the motion of Dr. White the following gentlemen were appointed a deputation : Major Pickmere, Messrs. J. Fairclough, Dakin, Tunstall and J. Roberts.
