WHAT may be regarded as a The Ship Canal counterblast to the statement of Again, Sir JOHN HARWOOD, with regard to the finances and general position of the Ship Canal, has been issued in the form of a report by Mr. J. K. BYTHELL, chairman of the Traffic and Rates Committee. There appears to be a pretty general opinion—in the Manchester direction at all events—that Sir JOHN HARWOOD has taken a far too pessimistic view of the prospects of the Canal. It is argued, and reasonably, that it is a long way too soon yet to declare the Canal a failure. Mr. BYTHELL'S report will, on Vie whole, strengthen this feeling. He points out that the Suez Canal was open five years before there was any large development of traffic, and this, indeed, has been the fortune of many other large and costly under- takings which are now considered to be very pro- fitable investments. The report deals mainly with three important points, namely, the traffic on the Canal since it was opened;the circumstances which have retarded the development of that traffic; and the prospects of improvement in the future. With regard to the first point, which is one upon which much public interest has been centred for some months past, Mr. BYTHELL says : The traffic on the Ship Canal for the five months ending May was as follows : Tons. Merchandise in seagoing vessel. 211,915 —23,888 Merchandise in barges 63,785...... 919 Ships dues Passengers (323,056) 3,469 Cattle (979) 5O

Total revenue from t9lls and dues

As regards working expenses, it may be said- broadly that the revenue for the five nionths will. more' than cover (1) all the expenses connected with. the traffic, including wages, salaries,and stores ;, (2) rates, (3) a pull proportion of head office expenses during the same period. Maintenanle is not in-' eluded ; until the works axe more complete I take it that all outlay on works will be a charge against capital.

This part of the report is, on the whole, more favourable than many people had anticipated, though of course, if the Canal is to be a commer- cial success there must be a 'great improvement in the future. With regard to the causes that have delayed the,developtnent of traffic, it is pointed out amongst other things that traders had.. been. sceptical as to whether the Canal would really be open at the beginning of the year, and had, con- sequently, .not made their shipping engagements ,with that event in view ; that dock appliances have been in an unfinished state;that abattoirs are needed for the American trade, cold storage for the products of Australia and New Zealand, and oil, tanks and large Warehouses for mineral oil and grain imports; and that merchants make their arrangements so far ahead that, for i•Astance, last seteion's cotton crop had been dealt with before ',the Canal was open for traffic, while another export season will not open till September next. The greatest difficulty of all has, however, been found in dealing with :the railway companies. 'These companies seem to still display that hostil-. ity to the Ganal which was so evident in the

, THE EX MINE S 11,1/1 Y, . JUNE 16 1894.

RUNCORN BOWLING CLUB.

A bowling match between the Runcorn Bowling Club and a team representing Verdin Park, NOrthwich, took place on Wednesday afternoon, at the Bridge Inn, Run- corn. The result of the encounter was that Buncorn's total score in the 'single's" was 205 out of a possible 210, whilst in the "double's" they scored 102 out of a possible 105. The scores of their opponents were 94 in the singles and 48 in the doubles, thus leaving Runcorn in possession of a splendid victory.

===== 0002601_18940616_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
Ivarious Parliamentary inquiries. As Mr. BYTHELL says, "Until the railway companies agree to frame their rates for the Manchester dock traffic on sub- stantially the same basis as those in force at the other ports in competition with Manchester, the Ship Canal cannot get any of the traffic of the surrounding towns—cannot become a distributing centre." A hope is expressed that the railway companies will come to terms, but if they do not an appeal will be made to the Railway Com- missioners. This is a difficulty which will, undoubtedly, be overcome. The railway com- panies are powerful corporations, but they are not aibpowerful, and the forces, commercial and , political, which can be exercised on behalf of the canal are too influential to permit of the " boycot " being permanently maintained. With regard to the prospects of the Canal, Mr. BYTHELL points out that steps are being taken, partly icy the Canal Company, partly by the Manchester Corporation, and partly by private enterprise,to provide the equipment that is neces- sary. The Manchester Corporation will erect lairages at Mode Wheel and provide cold storage for imported beef and mutton ; and amongst pri- vate enterprises which will bring grist to the Canal Company, the, intention of the Whole- sale Co-operative Society to build wapworks at Irlam, and of RICHARD EVANS AND COMPANY, LIMITED,tO construct a coal port at Acton Grange are mentioned. In conclusion, Mr. BYTHELL assures us that the difficulties that have been met with " were not by any means unexpected, and they can be removed within reaso-• able time, but it is not to be expected that Manchester can attain the position of a second Liverpool in a few months' time, before even the Canal is completed to its full depth." ,

. IN several respects, New Zealand Women Voters.seems to be the most advanced of

English speaking communities This week we have had two meetings in Warring- ton which aptly illustrate the prominent position which women are now taking and the influence they exert in political and temperance questions. But in this country they are still under many dis- abilities as compared with men. In New Zealand they exercise equal political rights with their brothers and their husbands. The results of the .extension of the franchise to women in the Anti- podean colony are interesting and instructive. As Mrs. STEWART BROWN remarked at the meeting of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association on the opinion is often expressed that if women were given the vote they would vote Conserva- tive, but this opinion is not supported by the ex- periences of New Zealand. At the first general election after the extension of the franchise a thoroughly Radical government was returned with a large majority behind it. On temperance, too, a crucial question there as it is here, the women voters have thrown their influence on the side of the reformers. A Local Veto Bill, under another name, is in force in New Zealand, and re- cently a poll has been taken in all the electoral districts to decide whether public houses should be closed altogether, reduced in number, or left as they were. The result, accord- ing to the latest information, is a great victory for temperance. In some of the districts total pro- hibition has been voted, although a majority of three-fifths was required for the purpose, and in most of the other districts the number of licenses has been 'reduced by twenty-five per cent.

SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARIES AT WIDNES.

On Sunday the anniversary in connection with the Deacon-road Baptist Sunday School was celebrated. In the morning and evening sermons were preached by the Rev. G. Davies, D.D.. principal of Bangor College, and in the afternoon an address to scholars, teachers and friends was given. Speciol hymns were sung at each service.—The anniversary in connection with Stewards- lane Wesleyan Sunday School, Ditton, was also cel,.- brated on Sunday. Special sermons were preached morning and evening and in the aft.rnnon a service of song was admirably rendered by the choir.

RUNCORN RAMBLING CLUB.

About thirty-two members of the Ramblin!, Club con- nected with the Brunswick Sunday S3hool,Runcorn,had their third excursion this season on Saturday afternoon, their destination being Lymtn.- They started from Brunswick-st. at about two o'clock in wagonettes, sup- plied by Messrs. Booth and Sons,Bridge-st. On arriving' at their journey's end an excellent tea was partaken of, after which the party proceeded to visit the most in- teresting places. Having thoroughly enjoyed themselves they at length returned home, which was reached at half-past nine. RUNCORN CYCLISTS: A SPIN TO NEWTON„

The Wednesday wheelmen connected with the Runcorn Cycling Club had another pleasant run on Wednesday, when they had a most enjoyable spin to Newton. The outward journey being completed, they indulged in a flame of bowls at the Oak Tree Hotel whilst tea was being prepared. At the conclusion of tea an imprompnt concert was given by the members in excellent style, after which an hour was spent on the lake, this item of the programme proving I;4y refreshing. rhe homeward run was shortly afterwards commenced, the distance being covered in nice time.

ANNIVERSARY OF THE BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN.

By reference to our advertising columns it will be seen that the anniversary services in connection with the above school will take place on Sunday morning,' and evening next, in the St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel, Run- corn, when eloquent sermons will he preached by the W. L. Watkinson, of London.. On Monday evening public meeting will be held, when the rev. gentleman alreadyßev. named, and Mr. John Simpson, will deliver addresses on Sunday school work. It is hoped that the attendances will b., large, and that the appeals for sup- port will be liberally responded to.

RUNCORN OCCASIONAL POLICE CASES.

On Wednesday morning before Mr. F. J. Norman, at the Runcorn Police Court, Samuel Je,ffs, labourer, of Kinnsley, was charged with being drunk and disorderly ira High-st., Runcorn, on the night of the 12th inst.— Evidence in support of the charge was given by P.C. Gregory.—Defendant denied being drunk, and on being questioned said he had only had four pints of beer that day, which he considered would not make him into:gi- eated, inasmuch as it required about thirty pints to pro- duce that effect. (Laughter.) Defendant was fined 10.3. and costs, or 14 days' imprisonment.—At the &amp Courthouse on Thursday morning, before Messrs. P. Speakman, C.C., and S. Taylor, Katherine MeConn, re- siding in Water-st., was sentenced to 14 days' without, the option of a line, for being drunk and disorderly in Water-st., Runcorn, on the night of the 13th inst.

RUNCORN BOATING CLUB.

_ Mr. E. H. Kniveton presided over the general tneeting of the Runcorn Boating Club, which was held on Friday evening at the Cocoa Rooms, for the election of officers forming rules, Sc.c. There was a good attendance of members, and the proceedings were fairly enthusiastic. Mr. Fred Abel was elected captain: Mr. J. H, West, sub-capta'n ; secretary and treasurer, Mr. W. A. Ma- linger. The following gentlemen were appointed a com- mittee :—Messrs. E. McNorton, Eric Speakman, J. S. Smith, D. Coventry, and E. H. Kniveton, A code of rules was drawn up and agreed to. The members resolved that a wooden boathouse on the banks of the Weaver Canal, Rock Savage, be constructed. It was stated that having bought two boats there was a balance of £5 in hand. SUDDEN DEATH OF A CHILD AT RUNCORN. , An inquiry was conducted on Saturday afternoon at the Runporn Court flouse by Mr.Dobson,deputy coroner, relative to the sudden death of a child eleven weeks old named Hannah Marson, daughter of Joseph Marson, captain of the narrow boats "Frank " and " Lizzie."— The evidence of the mother, Eliza:. Marson, was to the effect that the child was a healthy "one but rather small. It was not insured. At about ten o'clock on Thursday evening her husband, herself, and the baby slept on board the "Frank." When she awoke at six o'clock the following morning the child was lying quite dead upon her right arm. The child's face though turned towards her was not very close. Upon being questioned she stated that she had had nothing to drink of an intoxica- Vng nature until night when her husband, herself, and the mate drank a quart of beer between them. Her husband was sober and the slide of the cabin was half way open.—The deputy coroner, having stimmd up the evidence, in course of which he said that the cabin ceemed a very improper place for three to sleep in, the jury returned a verdict of "accidentally overlain by its mother." A RUNCORN COMMISSIONER THROWN FROM HIS HORSE. About eight o'clock on Monday morning, ikMr, Walter Collier, grocer, of Church-3treet, Runcorn, diet with a serious accident. He was riding from Weston to Run- corn On one of his horses, when on arriving at the slide, Higher Runcorn, where the roadway is laid with very hard stones, the animal stumbled tind fell throwing Mr. Collier, with much force to the ground. The latter was severely cut on the forehead, and one of his arms and a wrist were sprained. Subsequently, he was attended by Dr. McLennan, and is mow recovering in a satisfactory manner.

SERIOUS ACCIDENT AT THE SPRINCII WORKS, RUNCORN. An accident of a somewhat serious nature occurred on Wednesday evening to Alfred Stubbs, labourer' 40 years of age, and residing at Gladstone Terrace, Runcorn. Whilst engaged on a stage connected with a barge at the Sprinch Shipbuilding Works, he fell into the dry dock, a distance of nine feet, and his head struck one of the stone steps. He was picked up and immediately con- veyed to his home where he was attended by Dr. McDougall, who certified that he was suffering from con- cussion ofithe brain. This (Friday) morning he was pro- gressing satisfactorily. ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN BAND OF HOPE, RUNCORN. ANOTHER CASE OF SMALLPDX AT ST. riELENS. The medical officer of St. Helens (Dr. Robertson) reported to the Health Committee on Wednesday that there had been another case of smallpox, the patient being a relative of the man who imported the disease six weeks ego,and who had died. Scarlet fever cases were diminishing in number, and there had only been eighteen cases during the three weeks. In the month of May the female sanitary inspector had made 971 visits in the poorer districts of Gerard's Bridge, Pocket Nook,- and Greenbank.

THE PROPOSED GIUVINU DOCK FOl

RtiNCO RN. DEPUTATION OF SHIPWRIGHTS, kc. The Runcorn Improvement. Commissioners held a special meeting on Wednesday evening in the Board room, Town Hall, for the purpose of receiving a deputation of shipwrights, painters, and boiler- makers, with reference to the question of the pro- posed provision of a graving duck on the banks of Manchester Ship Canal, at Runcorn. The chair was occupied by Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., and the Commissioners present were Messrs. P. 6peakman, J.P., C.C., J. Prit- chard, W. Fisher, W. Handley, D. Bisbrown, W. Leach, E. Imison, H. J. Grice and 'l. Lea with Mr. F. A. Lake. law clerk ; and Mr•E. Marshall,secretary. The member.: of the deputation were Messrs. J, Bayles, J. Boyle, J. 1 Hitchin, A. Ravenscroft, G. Kirkham (shipwrights) t C. Stubbs, J. Stubbs, (master shipwrights) ; William lox- worth, J. Rathbone (painters) ; J. Hayes (master painter) ; J. Senior, N. Senior (boiler makers) ; and H. Branch (master boiler maker). 'rue Chairman stated, when the deputation were ad- mitted, that he did not consider it wise to conduct the proceedings in the presence Of the Press. Consequently ' the meeting was held in private. From certain sources of information, however, we have been able to gather that the torm et the proceedings was as follows. The cl.putation being invited by the Chairman to state their _

Mr. Hitchin opened by saying that the ship-building and repairing industry in Runcorn bad, in consequence of the construction of the Ship Canal, been praccically swept away. There were 220 men on the books of the Shipwrights' Society' previous to the making of the canal, but at the present time that number had considerably diminished. This proved that the statement sometimes made to the effect that more men were now employed was false. The suggestion to provide slipways would not meet the requirements so well as a graving dock, because the itraio upon the ship wreathed the coppers, and seriously prejudiced the value of the vessel in the estimation of insurance companies. He hoped the Com- missioners would stand firm to the agreement and expressed the opiniva that grain warehouses, etc, would not benefit the town as much as a graving dock. Mr. Hitchiu went on to say that one of the questions asked on chartering a vessel was "what facilities are there at the port of destination for there- pair of ships ?" and if there was no such accommodation then those places were avoided as much as possible by charterers. He spoke of several cases which had comel under his personal notice in which ships kad brought CA r• goes to Runcorn, then had to go to Livcrpool for repairs and afterwards return to Runcorn to load._

Mr. S:ubbs supported the contentions of Mr. Hitchin, and supplemented them by saying that it would be very convenient if a small graving dock was opened opposite Bridgewater House to accommodate vessels of 100 tons burden. He cited many difficulties experienced at the present time in the way of repairing vessels and pointed out an instance in which they tried to beach a ship in the river for repairs. A hole was made but the tide came up and filled it with sand. This operation was repeated, but with the same result and ultimately the workmen bad to go inside andttop the breach. Mr. Kirkham dealt with the que,tion as to Whether a. graving dock would pay if they spent so much upon one. Wuld the charges remunerate them for the cost? He contended tha'.; they would, and quoted a table of rates levied in Liverpool, and showed that the charges varied from a few pounds to £250 for finding accom- modation for a vessel in the graving dock. _ _

Mr. Boyle and Mr. Branch' both-urged the Commis- sioners to stick to the agreement. Tim latter gentle- than referred to the loss of trade to the town through not being able to execute certain work owing to their not having a graving dock. Mr. Lake produced the agreement with the company, and also the plan of the proposed graving dock. The former he read, after which he entered into explanation with reference to the correspondence which had p tssed between the Commissioners and the Company. The deputation manifeited great interest in the plan, which they closely inspected. They subsequently retired to a- room above for the purpose of discussing the mat- ter. They afterwards returned and

Mr. Hitchin stated that they had considered the sub- ject very carefully and were still convinced that the graving dock was the best thing for them. They prefer- red that it should_ be on the western side of 'Runcorn Bridge rather than in the position shown upon the plan. A Commissioner ask d the deputation whether a. pontoon would not meet their requirements, to which a reply was made that it would not. It was explained that accommodation would have to br made for a pon- toon, in which it was not s. pleasant for the men to work. They were not so certain of a dry plain upon which to work in a pontoon, and to have a graving dock would he a di.trict advantage over a pontoon or slip- ways. Mr. Hitchin said the deputation were perfectly satisliel with the way in which tho Commissioners had received them, and were convinc:d that they had done everything they could for the best interests of the town.

The Chairman repliel that the representations brought forward by the deputation would receive the most care- ful consideration. The Commissioners were quite alive to the importance of resuscitating the languishing indus- try, and also to the tact that a port was not properly equipped unless it had provision for repairing the ships which frequented it. Mr. Pet:hard thought it would not be advisable to strongly press the Canal Company at the present time, in view of their present difficulties. Mr. Speakman spoke favourably on the idea of imme- diate representation to the company. It was not a question of expediency but one of bargain. He was pleased to find that the agreement was a binding one, and one which the Commissioners would he able to insist upon being carried out. No other idea than the provi- sion of a graving dock should be entertained. He con- tended that as the company would have to go to Parlia- ment for mlre money they might just as well include the expenditure for smith in their estimate. After a few more obiervations from various speakers the proceedings coecluded.

"A SAILORS' GRAVE " IN RUNCORN CEMETERY.

AN OBJECE DESEPSING PUBLIC SYMPATHY.

Mr. William Shaw, the well-known and respected missionary connected with the Runcorn branch of the Mersey Nlission to Seamen, has formulated a scheme in the carrying out of which, ha certainly deserves the true sympathy of all, and more especi- ally of seamen. For some years he has been cogni- zant of tie want of a special grave for the interment of se tinen unfortunately drowned in the locality, and far away from their homes. The necessity hag been brought under his notice innumer able times throuen the parents relatives, or friends of deceased seamen inquiring from him where the body of some relative )r friend has been deposited in the cemetery. It is known that at the pre- sent time the bodies of such are interred. and, of course in the majOrity of cases, no stone or anything is erected t) denote who is buried beneath. This being the case, Mr. Shaw suggested to the Committee of the Mission at Liverpool, that a plot of land should be bought in the ltimwrn Cemetery, wide enough to admit of foNor graves side by side, sufficient for twenty interments; and that the land •be specially reserved for the burial of all seamen found drowned in the Runcorn district. He also suggested that a monu- ment be erected thereon, and that the names of all in- terred seamen. where it was possible to procure them, be inscribed on it, and that the grave he called " 'tne Sailors' Grave." The committee inquired into the sub- ject. and the matter was also laid betore the Archdeacon , of Macclesfield. Chaplain of the Missi:m, with the result that the consent has just been received to the suggestion trine' carried into eir-ic. The cost, including the lend, monument. Sz., will be £3O. which sum Mr. Shaw hones to raise with the assistance of his Runcorn friends.- The monument has been already desizned, this having been carried Omit hy Captain Crofton,or II MS. "Eagle," who has manifested considerable interest in .the matter. It is proposed to start a fund to dofray the cost of the un- dbrtakin,A and it is to be hoped that toe appeal for sip- port will not be made in vain. •

ALARMING FIltE AT RUNCORN.

A DRAPERY ESTABLISHMENT GUTTED.

I A fire, of a most destructive character; occurred at an early hour on Wednesday morning on the premises of 57, High-street Runcorn, occupied at that time by Mr. D. 11. Jones, droper,&c. On Tues- day evening Mr. Jones and his son William both re- tired to bed leaving everything apparently sale. Shortly after half-past two o'clock in the morning, the father was awakened by a crashing sound. He went into the adjoining bedroom to his son whom he found had likewise been awakened by the sound. To him he expressed the opinion that burglars had entered the shop. Both went downstairs and upon Mr. Jones open- ing the shop door he saw one corner of the establish- ment was a mass of fire. He rushed upstairs in order to descend by a staircase leading into the back of the house, whilst his son ran to the police station and gave the alarm. The notification was received by, P.C.Wright, who passed on word to Sergeant Lee, and the latter aroused Superintendent Atherton and Inspector Hicks. The Sergeant accompanied by P.C. Butler ran to the Sprinch Works, and in attempting to open the gate the key broke in the lock which resulted in the loss of two or three minutes. The gate, however, was climbed,the " buzz.,,r "sounded, and quickly several members of the brigade were on the spot. About seventeen minutes after the buzzer was blown, Deputy-Superintendent Wright, Sergeant William Brown and ten men reached the scene of the fire with the hose-cart and reel. They promptly connected the pipes with the mains, and in a short time a vast volume of water was being poured upon the flamc.e; which had, by this time, as- sumed, alarming proportions and presented a magnifi- cent sight. A perfect hold had now been taken by the tire upon the large and valuable stock in the shop. A number of neighbours rendered assistance and at about four o'clock the brigade had secured the mastery of the flames. They were not extinguished however until nearly six o'clock, and tor several hours afterwards a watch was kept upon the still smouldering embers by two or three firemen in case of a furttft outbreak. The water supply was very plentiful. Superintendent Ather- ton, Inspector Hicks. Sergeant Lee, and a number of constables did good service in co;_ing with the conflagra- tion, as well as in the maintenance of order.

It has been stated that the men engaged at the adjoin- ing works had detected the smell of burning timber,but had been unable to discover the source from whence it issued. It is apparent, therefore, that the fire must have, been smouldering for sometime, and the escape of Mr. Jones and his son is considered extremely providential inasmuch as they occupied rooms at the front of the house, and directly ever the shon. The excitement created by the exten- sive fire increased tenfold, owing to the shop of Messrs. Lea being next door, in which was stored a vast quan- tity of petroleum, benzoline, matches, candles,and other materials of a highly inflammable nature. Consequently the efforts of the fire brigade were chiefly directed against the fire near this .shop.ancl fortunately they were successful. If, however,the flames had once penetrated into Mr. Lea's establishment,nothing possible could have prevented the destruction of the whole block of valuable buildings.

The cause outbreak of the fire is unknown, but a sus- nieion is abroad that some smoker, in passing,may have - dropped a lighted mAtch into some of the material at the clocr previous to the closing of the shop, and that this material has been taken into the shop v.,ithout notice being dr4wii to its perhaps smouldering state. The gas in the shop was not liolited, nor was there any fireplace near to where the fire is supposed to have originated. Very few articles were saved and Mr. Jones estimates his loss at £1,500. Towards this he holds a policy of insurance in the Alli- ance Company for £1,200. The 'building, which belongs to the executors of Messrs. flazlehurst is said to be damaged to the extmt of £3OO. This is also covered by insurance in the London, Liverpool, and Globe Company. Mr. Jones has received many expressions of sympathy through his serious loqs, and has, at the same time, been congratulated upon his narrow escape from an untimely death. Mrs. Jones and her daughter were away at the time spending a holiday at Holywell.

LIMITED. LLANELLT. SOUTH WALES

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

SUSP ICIOUS CONDUCT.

At the Warrington BorQugh Court on Thursday morning, three young women named Teresa Weave, 7, Dakin's Court, Mary Ireland, 7, Dakin's Court, and Elizabeth Staners, 6, Dakins Court, were brought before the magistrates. The first named defendant was charged with being a convicted thief, and in company with the other prisoners found frequenting the market with in- tent to commit a telnny. The Chief Constable (Mr. Bunt) informed the Bench that on the previous after- noon, Detective Monaghan was in the market and he saw the three prisoners together. He watched their movements fox about an hour, and also obtained the assistance of Mr. Robert Lewis to watch theta also. Gleave, who .was well-known by the police, was seen to go amongst a crowd, and put her hand in a woman's pocket. The three, who were acting in concert, were brought to the police station. He (Mr. Hunt) was pre- sent when they were brought in, and on Cileave being asked to deliver up what money she had, she declared she had neither purse nor money. On being searched a purse containing 3s. 10d. was found upon her. They had not yet. however, been able tA find the owner of the purse. He asked for a remand in the case of Gleave, and would leave the magistrates to deal with the other two prisoners. Detective Monaghan and Mr. R. gave corroborative evidence. Inspector Spinks said no money,w3 s found by the female searcher on the defendant Ireland, and only a penny was found upon Staners. The magistrates remanded Gleave,and ordered the other two defendants to be bound over to appear when called upon.

SHOCKING CRUELTY TO A POSY.

Martha Moores. greengrocer, 25, Granville-street, Runcorn, was charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on Thursday moftinty„ with having cruelly ill- treated a pony by working it in an unfit state at the Market, the previous afternoon. Inspector ',tiff, R.S.P.C.A., said at 12 o'clock the previous day he was in Market-street when he saw a pony which attracted his attention by its miserable appearance. It was lame on both hind legs, and there was also a large sore on the back, about seven inches long, causing it much pun. He waited for the owner to turn up for about an hour, but as no one came it was taken to the Police Station. The r.,acking which coveren its back was removed, and the part which had been pressing upon the sore was saturated with blood and matter. Later in the day the defendant came to the Police Station to claim the pony. and on being questioned she said she had brought it from Runcorn that morning. It was an old pony and in a very poor condition. P.C. Clough said lie removed the pony from the market to the PoEce Station. lie had very great difficulty in bringing it, as the animal could scarcely walk. Inspector Spinks said the animal was totally unfit for work, and its back was like a piece of raw beef- Sergeant Chantler also gave evidence. The Chairman said he had seen the pony and it could .scarcely stand. He was surprised that the defendant tad been allowed to bring it out of Runcorn. The -animal was ordered to be destroyed, and the defendant was dismissed with a caution.

WARRINGTON AND THE DOCKS.

THE MUNICIPAL FINANCE QUESTION.

" I have not," said the gentleman designated the "Busy Bee," in reference to Councillor J. Shaw Green's final statement of last week, in connection with the Municipal Finance discussion, " seen the Borough Treasurer's report for 1894, and therefore we have only the 1893 report to go on. It was on the figures contained in that report,that 1 based my calculations and arrived at the average rate of interest paid by the Corporation, viz., .E 3 6s. What has been done since in paying off all the :C3 10s. and loans has nothing to do with the Borough Treasurer's report for 1893. Councillor Shaw Green referred to a Select Committee of the House of Commons reporting in 1882 that they had in no case permitted the repayment of a loan to be extended beyond 60 years. When I quoted Reading as having the very year before got more than this period, he then says it is since 1882. "I have this week been in communication with the Local Government Board and they state that since 1882 several towns certainly have obtained periods longer than 60 years for the repayment of their loans.

"The fact is the Finance Committee have no money and they don't know which way to turn to find it. On the one hand they are frightened of tke ratepayers—and well they might be with their 6s. Bd. rate f—and on the other hand they appear to be frightened out of their very wits of the Local Government Board

"What do we want money for? And how can we get it cheapest? Those are the questions which want the attention of the Council. We want money to make Warrington what it aught to be and what it deserves to be.

"We want money for the Warrington docks, for it is evident the Canal Company cannot make them, Sir John Harwood having even said in respect to the question that "you cannot squeeze blood from a stone." We also want money to widen the thorough- fares block by block. "We want the money without increasing the burden of taxation. The vice-chairman of the Finance Committee and the members of that Com- mittee have been told in plain and uncaistakelble. terms that they are borrowing their money too dear and repaying it too quickly and that through their want of tact the ratepayers are heavy losers ! that if they are going to Parliament the ir better plan is to go for powers to issue a consolidated stock charged' indifferently on all their rates leviable, &c.' and on, the funds end revenues belonging to the Corpora- tion, and above all not to adopt those methods of finance that have been discarded by other Corpora- tions as disadvantageous, namely the issuing of 'de- benture stock." "I cannot say with Councillor Shaw Green what the Committee of the House will do. I only know that to have tine broad streets and thoroughfares ' through Warrington would be a good thing and that to have the Warrington docks so that vessels of 3,000 tons could berth there without encountering A lock on their journey from the sea would be a great blessing to the manufactures of Warrington, and through them indirectly, to the ratepayers .at large. "I know the scheme of carrying out these improvements can be put into effect.. It simply: wants the will to do it, Granted that the Bill has to be deposited by the 20th December, there is nothing to prevent it being deposited by the Ist October, and this being so very little time can be lost in drafting a comprehensive scheme and arranging for the counsel, deputations, and wit- nesses to attent Parliament.

"Bankers, merchants and manufacturers came into the town in view of the advent of the Manchester Ship Canal and it, is useless- to urge them to wait. The Ship Canal Company must know that the mcmey they have in hand will I be absorbed in the erection of suitable warehouses at Manchester, the lack of which is at the present time preventing the ship owners from taking their vessels thither for discharging their cargoes.. In the meantime Liverpool is bidding against Man-, chester for the traffic, and they are getting it, and will continue to get it, whereas if many of the vessels were able to put into the port of -Warrington the Manchester Ship Canal Comp.ny would at any rate gain the advantage of the tolls from Warring- ton to Eastham. "It is therefore to the interests of 'both Warring- tem and the Canal Company that they should come to terms, and provision be made by Warrington for the construction of the docks. _

" 'rainy knowledge manufacturers of Warrington have not been interviewed wit's a view to ascer- taming what traffic they could guarantee the Canal Company, and I believe, despite opinions to the contrary, that if suitable terms were made sufficient traffic could he guaranteed from Warring- ton to ensure the success of the docks. _ .

"The Ship Canal Company. I: understand.have expressed their willingness to help the Corporation. in every way if they are desirous of con4ructing their own docks. They have offered to give War- rington their sanction ,and approval if they seek to apply for the power to undertake-the work, which could be included in the Bill for the widening of the streets. This work shoula be got on with at onee,.inetead of allowing two years and a halfito elapse which cannot fail to retard the progress of the town, and divert the traflialrora the Canal Coml. . party..

THE ARNIS OF WARRINGTON.

Sir,—l have lately been endeavouring to compile, under the title of "The Book of POlic Arms," a collec- tion ol municipal and county arms. I should be glad if you would allow me through your columns to draw public attention to the arms of your town. These, as they have been supplied to me, are described in the accompanying cutting -in-1m my proof sheets, but as the arnss of the Corporation, they are absolutely bogus and of no legal or valid authority. Further, the - presumption which is made to the accompanying ornaments of the banners of the Ea,ridoin of Chester and the Duchy of Lancaster is at the same time impudent and supremely ridiculous. I understand that recently, through the generosity of Mr. F. Monks, a handsome pair of gates for the entrance of the new park have lately been presented to the town, and I hear that it is intended that this aforesaid ludic- rous achievement should appear in all its bastard glory in a prominent position thereupon. May I enter my protest against any such perpetration of sheer ignor- ance? It would be far and away better to have DO arms at all than that insignia should beset up which any- one having the least heraldic knowledge can turn at once into an object of open ridicule. Considering the importance of the town of Warrington, it most certainly would seem to be desirable that someone intimately or officially connected should take steps to see that a legitimate coat of arms were obtained to take the place of the bogus achievement in use. There is no necessity for the use of any arms at all, but if the display i 3 made, the laws of arms most certainlyshould be complied with, and a genuine coat be substithited.—Yours,

ARTHUR CHARLES FOX-DAVIES. Granville House, Arundel-stre?t, London, W.C. The following is the cutting referred to above :—War- ringtm (Lanca,shire)—Has no Armorial Bearings. The following are quoted in Burke's " General Armory' : " Ar. six lioncels, three, two, and one gu.. being the Arms of Vilars, the first Lords of Warrington." The Corporation generally make use of the design upon the seal, which represents the above arms, and behind them in saltire two banners,that on the dexter si 1e gules, charged with three lions passant guardant in pale or,and a label of three points argent, being intended for the arms of the county Palatine of Lancaster ; that on the sinister side azure charged with three garbs or, being intended for the arms of the County Palatine of Chester. In place of the crest, the seal shows a sword and a mace in saltire, the sword enfiled with a wreath of laurel,tloth surmounted by .a scroll hearing the ins:!ription," Anno Decimo Victoria: Reginse." Debrett gives a motto (which appears to be used in the t3wo), " Deus clat incremen- turn."

0! THE PITY OF IT!

Sir,—l have lately been entrapped into the disburse- ment of a sum of money-2;0 to the £—for which I consider myself not to have received an equivalent—as advertised. In a moment of weakness I invested the sum of one penny in a copy of a paper called the "Anti- Infidel," attracted by the title of one of its articles, " Moses more reliable than the astronomers." Immedi- ately my imagination went out upon the subject and conceived some strong hand, directed by some strong head, rehabilitating the character of Moses as a scientifi- cally accurate historian. Need I say that it would give me pleasure to see the astronomers" slated "—that is the appropriats slang,l believe—if their divergence from Mosaic accuracy would be demonstrated ! In pleasant anticipation of the act of recuperative justice I spent my penny and lost my pleasant anticipation in the blank and disappointing reality. Naturally, where the alleged inaccuracies of a science like astronomy are concerned, one looks for physical de- monstration of the alleged errors. But no! From be- ginning to end the article deals only in the opinions of such people as "Parallax," or Dr. Shoepter, of Berlin, who is of opinion that the preser.t sys'!em of astronomy is an " universally disseminated delirium of lunatics!' No evidence is offered in support of such statements of which the article is full, but the world—the believing world—the anti-infidel world—is expected to accept such assertions upon the ipse clixit of a set of cranks. And here is the pity of it ! Let us assume, for argu- ment's:sake, that the bible is all that its admirers claim for it the Word of God divinely inspired by Him, and designed for the improvement and regeneration of man- kind. Let us concede, upon the same grounds, the dictum of another luminary, that the bible is " the most scientific book in existence. It is loaded with science from the first chapter of Genesis to the end of the Revelation." Let all this be granted for the titne,would it not, have been more convincing and less of an insult to the understanding of the readers of the "Anti-Infidel" in general, and of this most remarkable article of William Bathgate,M.B.C.A., in particular,if he had been able to " show up " even one of the errors of modern astronomy? To assume that the God who designed this work needs to have it defended by such meretricious futilities is an insult and an impiety against the God they profess to adore. Such stuff as this can only result in intellectual degradation to its readers, and, although I feel sure it can only tend to advance the cause I have at heart—the freedom of religion from theological train- mels—still it grieves me to see even so good a cause advocated by such wretched means. Surely it is punish- ment enough for the believers to accept the inevitable, to be slowly, but surelyi put through the agonizing prn— cess of disillusion, without the added injury of such intellectual insults ! The cause of the God of Truth can never be advanced by meritricious means. Viewed from any reasonable and reasoning stand-point such fallacious methods of promoting such objects are simply and entirely deplorable ; but it is a state of things en- couraged and cultivated by a priesthood more intent upon fostering the emotional than the intellectual side of human nature. Oh ! the pity of it.—Yours. Scr!.. JOE-IN ITEMS.

THE RECTOR AND THE DRINK TRAFFIC: THE VICAR OF BURTON WOOD ON THE RECENT

CO'STROVE EISY.

Sir,—Some unknown friend has had the kindness to send me through the post a copy of the " Burtonwood and Newton-le-Willows Church and Parish Magazine " for the present month,in which the Vicar of that parish, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, M.A.. makes some very frank and trenchant remarks on the recent controversy in your columns. Evidently the controversy has excited a good deal of attention and has been the cause of no little heartsearching amongst a good many Church people. If all the clergy were as outspoken as the Vicar of Burton- wood we should soon hear less of the scandal of the unholy alliance between the Church and the "'Trade." Believing that his remarks will be read with widespread interest by your many readers, I have ventured to send a copy of them in the hope that you will publish them— and so give them the wider publicity they so well de- serve. Thanking you in anticipation.—l am, yours truly,

JOHN GANDY.

Of the argument from Scripture wedo not think much —it is best to he candid—lt may he our ignorance which makes us unappreciatir e—anyhow we fail to see the logic of it—if there is logic in the argument. * * * *

When a custom however lawful and right, and even apparently backed up by Holv Scripture, degenerates into a national curse, and admittedly becomes the great scourge of the world civilized and uncivil,z.d (especially the civilized) there should ho no dilly dallying about its temporary abandonment fur social, moral, and religious reasons. If this is true of the drink traffic,—as now permitted —if annually it first plunders and then slays its victims by tens of-thousands—its continuance is no longer iusti- fiable ; its recognition by the state is an immoral rrct, and the enriching thereby of the national exchequer is a sharing in ill gotten spoil. Sharp remedies are necessary in extreme cases. Sus- pend the traffic until it is put upon a licalthy corstruc- tive basis. In the twinkling of an eye a transformation will be wrought which will convince the most sceptical.

For Christians there is, in view of the wickedness lowing' so closely in the train of the liquor traffic, no middle course. The Pauline attitude admits of no com- promiie—" Wherefore if moat make my brother to offend, I will eat no flesh while the world standeth, lest I make my brother to offend." * * * *

The most pressing call is for Erpwery Disestablishment and Disendowment. The state should have no quilms of conscience about stepping in and applying some of the Brewers' Millions to secular education. free:librarirs. public parks and gardens, and those many other im- proving and uplifting things laid upon the New Parish Councils to inaugurate and maintain—A just application of public money.

CxcIET.

Another draw was the result of the first meeting of the Warrington first team with Sale, on Saturday. As this makes the fourth successive draw, ‘Varrington will be getting well used to them by this time. In the match on Saturday, Warrington were minus the services of Dr. H. Smith, who had been playing so briliiantly the pre- vious day for the Lancashire second eleven against the Middlesex second string. His place was however well filled by Mr. A. C. Maclaren who came from Liverpool along with Mr. S. M. Crosfield after taking part in the sensational match between Middlesex and Lancashire. On the other hand, Sale were without their county player, Mr. C. H. Benton.

The wicket on Saturday was very fast owing. to the heavy rains of the previous two days, and Sale were ex- tremely fortunate in going in first to bat. Another thing also in their favour was that Warrington did not field as well as usual, and the consequence was that for the loss of only four wicket, they had compiled 153 runs, and then an hour and a half before time, they put on the closure. Fullalove was far and away the highest scorer on his side, and in fact, in the match, for ha scored 59 and then carried out his bat. J. Cookson also caused a lot of trouble to the Warrington fielders with his score of 40. Leerning also put on a quarter of a century. As might be expected from the score which Sale put on, the bowling averages of the Warrington players, were considerably damaged. The frequent changes of bowl- ing may be imagined when no less than seven cf the Warrington team tried their luck with the ball, but only three—Bibby, Connor, and Bates—obtained any wicltr-ts. Bil,,by was the most successful of the Warrington bow- lers with two wickets for .30 runs. The other two were very expensive, Connor's wicket costing 30 runs, and Bates 43 runs. THE EXAMIN-Elt: SATURDAY, JUNE' 16, 1394

THE POST OFFICE ENCOURAGES THRIFT.

With a view to the further encouragement of thrift, the Post Office authorities are taking vigorous steps for making more widely known the advantages offered by the Post Office Savings Banks, not only to depositors, but also to those who desire to insure their lives or te make provision for old age. Preparations are making for something like a house-to-house distribution of hand- bills giving full details, including the new investment regulations under which the tf..)al sum that may be deposited in one year is raised to £5O and the amount of stock that may be purchased through the Savings Bank during the same period is extended to £2OO until the maximum of £5OO has been reached. Show cards have also been prepared on a very extensive scale, giving in more or less bold type similar information in a con- venient form for hanging up in factories, workshops, and cffices throughout the kingdom.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

On Tuesday afternoon the fortnightly meeting of the Board was held in the Workhouse, Dutton, under the chairmanship of Mr. J. Mclntyre. There were also present the Revs. J. W. N. Tanner, J. Ilanby, and Dr. Gastaldi; Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., D. 11. Jones, A. It. Norman, J. Weir, J. Stokes, H. H. Heywood, T. Handley, S. Yarwood, C. I'. Whitley, W. Whitlow, J. L. Jones, T. Lewis, T. Baxter, C.C., E. Aaron, J. W. Baguley, J. Parr, C. W. Davenport, S. Woodward, J. Brarvireth, Vv. Turner, with J. Ash- ton, clerk; G. Ashton, deputy-clerk.

THE DEATH OF MRS. JOHN WEIR.

Mr. John Weir rose to thank all the mernbers of the Board tor th.r!ir kindness and consideration in sending him a message of sympathy on the occasion of his sad bereavement. Be appreciated the action to the full,and thanked them all very much.

APPOINTMENT OF ASSISTANT ORGANIST.

The sanction of the Local Government Board was re- ceived to the appointment of Miss Cunnah as assistant organist at the Workhouse at a salary of £5 per annum. The Local Government Board considered her too young to be assistant matron.—On the moticn of Mr. Bran- dreth, seconded by Mr. Lewis, Miss Cunnah was elected to the post.

THE JOINING OF WARRINGTON DISTRICTS TO THE RIINCOUN

The Clerk stated that he had "attended the recent County Council Inquiry at Warrington as Clerk to the Board. He was not able to call the committee together which had been appointed at their last meetin,g,for con- sultation, owing to his having received the notice of in- quiry at such a late hour. There was no necessity,how- ever, for him to ask any questions of the meeting,as one of the County Council Committee, Mr. Earp, made the inquiries which he (the Clerk) had intended to make. The question of joining Latchford, Grappenball, and Thel wall to the Runcorn Union was fully discussed, and the residents who lived in these townships when called upon for testimony all expressed a wish to be joined to Runcorn. The committee retired to consider the matter, and upon their return they granted the order.—Mr. Brandreth said he understood that Warrington was going to appeal against the decision to the Local Government Board. He had heard that about; 200 cases of settle- inent,through the works connected with the Manchester Ship Canahhad been brought against Warrington,and if the Runcorn Board were going to have nose thrown upon them with Thelwall, Latchford, and Grappenhall, they would have to consider the subject. (Laughter.)— The Rev. J. Hanby : The anxiety of Warrington to re- tain the townEhips is, I think, a sufficient guarantee.— The Chairman expressed the same opinion.

RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

----- Mr. John Mclntyre presickd over the fortnightly meeting of the above Authority at the Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday afternoon. The remaining gentlemen present were—the Revs. J.W. N. Tanner, and J. Hanby ; Messrs. W. Turner, H. E. Heywood, T. Lewis, C. W. Devonport, E. Aaron, J. Stokes, J. Brandreth, T.Baxter, C.C., J.L. Jones, C. T. Whitley, 111. Whitlow, S. Woodward, J. Weir, 3. W. Baguley, J. Parr, with Messrs. J. Ashton, 'clerk ; G. Ashton, deputy clerk ; J. Farrington, nuisance inspector ; and Dr. Adams, medical officer of health. •

REMUNERATION OP THE CLERK.. The Clerk reported the receipt of a letter from the Local Government Board sanctioning the payment to him of £B5 for his services to the authority during the year ended the 25th March. It further stated that the application in respect of the special allowances was under the consideration of the Board.—Mr. Ashton re- marked that the special allowances were those noted to him, but if there was any hitch, then he would ask for the pay•ment of £B5 to be re-considered. The work done in the past year was equal to the work done during the three previous years by the late clerk. The legal work alone would have cost the Authority over £lOO accord- ing to legal charges.—The Chairman exprensed a hope that no hitch would occur.

THE SCARLET FEVER OUTBREATC AT WESTON. In a letter to Mr. Ashton on the 31st ult, Dr. Adams said with regard to the above subject that he was first made aware of the cases verbally by Mr. Far- rington about the 4th or sth May. He went to Weston from Warrington on Sunday, May 6th, and found that two children had undoubtedly had scarlet fever, but very slight and were in the peeling stage. He received assurances from Mr. Jones that the eldest daughter alone would look after the shop, and the mother would nurse the patients. On the strength of that assertion he made no recommendation for closing the shop. On May 22nd he received from Dr. Robinson, Runcorn, four notifica- tions of scarlet fever occurring in Mrs. Jones's family, including the two cases already mentioned. He, (Dr. Adams) visited the IL-Juse again on Monday, May 28th, and reported as usual to Dutton the fcllowing day. The parents admitted on the 6th May that they believed the disease was scarlet fever, but said it was so slight that they saw no necessity to call id medical aid. In this he could not condemn them, as the cases, at that time, bore out the parents' statement. He was first inclined to recommend that sec. 126 of Public Health Act, he used against them, but felt when he knew all the circumstances, that prosecution either under that sec- tion or under the Notificati,ina Act, would meet with little success.—The Chairman considered the statement a satisfactory one.—Mr. Aaron thought that the docter should have gone to Weston immediately he received the notice from Mr. Farrington. By so doinc, it might have prevented any chance of transferring the complaint to other persons. Two other boys had suffered.—Dr. Adams said that so far as his memory served him, he visited the house the day after hearing from Mr. Far- rington. -

MEDICAL REPORT. Dr. Adams reported that one case of scarlet fever had occurred at Preston Brook, one at Sevenoaks, and three at Budworth. He had visited and inspected all the above cases and issued all customary orders. He had since heard that the schools at Xston had been closed in consequence of measles awl whooping cough having broken out in the district.

THE ALLEGED INADEQUATE SUPPLY OF WATER TO FARMS. The Clerk stated that he had received a letter from ' Mr. Evans, agent of Sir Richard Brooke, Bart., asking if the Authority would give him an audience if he attended their meeting. He (the Clerk) had replied saying that if the matter had reference to Halton and Norton, Mr. EVaD5' best course was to see the Authority.—lt was decided to admit Mr. Evans in order that the members might hear his case.—Upon entering the room Mr. Evans referred to a discussion which had occurred at the last meeting, of the members regarding the supply of water to Manor House Farm and Marsh Gate Farm, in the township of Norton, and Halton Gate Farm, in the township of Halton. It had been stated, he said, that Inspector Farrington had received a letter from the county medical officer that the supply at the two first named farms was very bad and there was Tits supply at Halton Gate Farm. Mr. Evans contended that Mr. Farrington had not visited the latter farm for the last two years. TllO well, "or hole in the earth "as Mr. Farrington had called it, had been dried up in consequence of the cutting of the Ship Canal. In 1890 the water was analysed and found good and fit for domestic use. Up to . last January there were, four big receptacles which caught all the water from one side of the buildings. These, however, had been taken away by Mr. Whitlow's brother. The tecant was going to get others. Regarding the water from the pipe, Mr. Evans said he would only be too glad if it were analysed. The folks had drunk some of it and had not suffered. It was a downright falsehood to say there was no water supply to the farm. Mr. Farrington submitted that there was no supply to the farm within the meaning of the Public Health Act which klaid that the supply must be sufficient and wholesome. The water had been an t- lysed and condemned on the 10th March 1891 as " bad and not fit for domestic use." He wrote to Mr. Evans on the 24th March, 1891, calling his attention to the fact , that the water had been condemned.—Mr. Evans twice denied that the notice had been given to him. Certain members of the Board had stated that the authority had been defied by Sir Richard Brooke and himself. This was untrue. He had obliged the inspector in eve-Ty pos- sible way, and had written for the water at Halton Gate Farm to be analysed. No one was more desirous than himself to carry out the wishes of the Board. As to Manor House Farm, Sir Richard and himself had put themselves to great trouble in the endeavour to get a water supply. The spring in the wood had not been analysed but it had been drunk by children without ill effect. He wished the water to be analysed. 11 the authority could give him any suggestions he would only be too pleased to carry them out. It was not right, however, to say that the Authority was de- fied.—After some further discussion'Mr. Evans said that if the Authority could find a supply by expending 'money he would gladly repay them the money.—Mr. Wood- wood thought it was outside the provinee of the Board to give suggestions to landlords. He thought the Autho- rity were satisfied that Mr. Evans wished to do what he could to provide a supply and if he acted at once in that direction he would not hear any more from them. But if the supply remained as it was then the Authority would have to take action.—Mr. Farrington remarked that some of Mr. Evan's statements were not true.—A lengthy discussion ensued in the course of which Mr. Farrington asked what was being done with regard to eight or nine cottages at Astmore and two cottages near Manor Farm, the inmates of which had to drink water out of a running water course containing sewage.—Mr. Evans replied that suggestions had not been made to him to which Mr. Farrington answered that it was not his duty to give suggestions.—The Chairman said they would have to take action if necessary 'as they had a duty to perform.—The Rev. J. Hanby inquired if Mr. Evans refused to do more than had been done. He presumed that that Authority would not take action upon its own responsibility until they had a refusal from Mr. Evaus. What did Mr. Evans propose to do in these cases P—Mr. Evans said he would practically leave the matter in the hands of the Authority. He could not help himself. He thought it would be more satisfactory if the Authority would snand the sum they were autho- rised to do and he would repay it.—On the proposition of Mr. Heywood, it was resolved that Mr. Farrington should prepare a report as to the water supply to the three farms named, and also the supply to the Astmore houses which report he was instructed to lay before the Authority at their nSe,ct meeting. -

THE COAL TRADE.

SERIOU: OUTLOOK.

It is now said that the depression in trade has effected the coal trade to such an exteat that the coalowners representatives have taken the formal step of requesting that a meeting of the Board of Conciliation should be held to consider the desirableness of reducing the pre- sent rate of miners' wages. The miners' representatives are understood to hold the belief that the rules framed for the guidance of the Board of Conciliation have not been complied with, and it is suggested that the miners' representatives will not attend the proposed meeting. It is stated that the miners' representa- tives would agree to a reduction of 10 per cent. on the present rate of wages provided that 'the re- duction should be coupled with a condition that after the 10 per cent, had been taken off, the wages remain- ing should represent the minimum below which they should under no circumstances go. At a meeting in Glasgow on ‘Vednesday the Scotch coalowners decided to offer a determined resistance to the demand of the men for an advance of wages, and there is now no doubt that the miners will come out on strike on the 25th inst.

NARROW ESCAPE OF A FATHER AND SON FROM DROWN- ING.—On Wednesday evening at abut seven o'clock, a little boy named Thomas Butters, son of Mr. Thomas Butters, labourer, of Frodsham Bride, had a narrow escape as well as his lather, from drowsing. The boy was amusing himself by throwing stones into the river Weaver,between the stone bridge and the railway bridge when he overbalanced himself and fell in the water. By strug,,,ling he worked himself nearly into the middle of the river. His father's attention was drawn to his dangerous position by the screaming ot some women and children, and in endeavouring to rescue his son he fell also into the water. Another man now appeared on the scene and with the assistance of some other folks he succeeded in pulling both father and.son out of the water. They were subsequently attended and at the present time, the boy is qui,e well whilst the father is suffering, somewhat slightly, from the shock to the system.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

One of the best attended meetings of this society was held on Sunday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. T. Birchall, J.P.. who commented upon the growth of t•Le soeie:y since he last presided at the meeting. He said people of the present day were apt tA be pessimistic when comparing the present time with the past, but when he locked back over a considerable number ot years lie considered that the condition of the people was far better at the present time than in former days. Mr. Fisque, of Nlanchester,was the speaker for the afternoon and he delivered a telling address. basing his remarks on the words contained in Ist Kings, Bc, 17 and 18 verses. He referred to many men who had been filled with good intentions, but who had been unable to carry them out, and spoke of the importance of people having a good purpose in life. .Although a man might not be able to acc•mplish at he wished to do, he reminded his hearers, that God accepted the will for the deed, it hav- ing emanated from the man's heart. Mr.W.H. %Valling- ton was the soloist, and contributed two solos in an admirable manner. Messrs. T. Wallingtoo, and J. Irish also took part in the meeting. BOLD-STHEET WESLEYAN CHAPEL.

MINING RIGHTS IN SOUTH WALES.

IMPORTAN r DISCOVERY.

As a result of an investigation by the Cardiff Corpora- tion in the Records Office, the original grant of large areas of mineral territory in South Wales has been dis- covered, and it is said to show that the mineral rights were reserved to the Crown. As the private owners now levy head rents and royalties,the question arises whether these coalfields do not really belong to the National Exchequer. Within the next few days the matter will be brought before the House of Commons by Welsh inembep, who will urge investigation and, if necessary the appointment of a Royal Commission to call upon the present claimants to establish their titles.

DEATH OF THE LORD CHIEF JUSTICE.

Lord Coleridge, Chief Justice of England, died on Thursday night at his house in London. The Hon. Bernard Coleridge, the representative of the Attercliffe division of Sheffield, succeeds to the peerage. It is ex- pected that the Unionists will make another effort to capture the seat. Their candidate wa3 defeated at the general election,t he figures being—Coleridge, (L) 5,107; G.C. Smith (U), 3,963. The death of Lord Coleridge may lead up to other vacancies in the House of Com- mons. The position of Lord Chief Justice is almost certain to be conferred upon Lord Russell of Killowen, recently created a Lord of Appeal, and Sir John Rigby, now Attorney General, is most likely to be elevated to the Upper House as Lord of Appeal, which will cause a vacancy for For far. Mr. Reid, now Solicitor General, will probably succeed Sir J. Rigby as Attorney General, for which he will not require re-election,and Mr. Haldane is generally designated for the Solicitor Generalship, on which account he would have to offer himself for re- election at Haddington. Itr_Tr.4l SAD BOATING DISASTER. _- A BOAT WITH EIGHTY IRISH HARVESTERS CAPSIZED. •

===== 0002601_18940616_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRIBLE LOSS OF LIFE.

A Westport correspondent telegraphs that a terrible boating disaster, resulting in the loss of at least thirty lives, occurred on Thursday off that port. It is custom- ary at this season of the year for large numbers of ablt- bo-died Men of Achill Island to migrate to England and Scotland for the purpose of earning at barvesting,money to pay the d ebts which they incur during the winter. On Thursday morning, about eighty of these harvesters, as they are called, embarked at Achill to proceed to Westport Quay, when a sudden gust of wind caught the sails and caused the boat to capsize, throwing the whole of its occupants into the water, in full view of many per- sons on shore. A terrible scene ensued, the struggles of the unfortunate men being seen and their cries for help heard from the shore. Boats were at once launched,and every effort was made to save the unfortunate people, but more than half of them had disappeared from view before the first rescue boat reached the spot. Between forty and fifty men were saved by the shore boats, but the remainder, numbering at least thirty, perished.

THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on Thurday evening, Sir G. 0. MonoAN moved that the Committee on the Great Western Rail way (No. 1) Bill should be instructed to strike out of the bill so much thereof as authorised the construction of the works described as Railway No. I and Railway No. 2. He contended that both from the local point of view and also as regarded access to Man- chester and other towns in Lancashire, the Great Wes- tern Company's sc heme was not so convenient as the proposed East Denbighshire line. MonoAN's motion was rejected by 156 to 114. Sir U. KAY-SHUTTLE WORTH, in reply to a queStion,said that Her Majesty's ship Bramble had been ordered to Tangier, and other sh ips would be within reach, in case of need, for the protection of the lives and property of British subjects. IS MARRIAGE A FAILURE ? What a question to ask,. but how often could it be answered in the affirmative, the principal cause being the improvidence of Furnishing on the Hire System. Since our advertisements have appeared pointing out the disadvantages. we have re- ceived several le ters agreeing with our denunciations of this pernicious sys'em. Each Hire Agreement specifies that after the deposit is paid, so many weekly payments shall be made and that until the whole of the sum mentioned is paid the goods shall not become the property of the would-be pur- chaser, and that if any of the payments are neglected to the exact time specified the goods may be forcibly taken away and the whole of the payments forfeited. County Court judges have denounced the sptem, inasmuch as the vendor ' gets both money and goods. Again, the prkes charged are enormous, without any respect being paid to the quality, in many cases being worn out before they are paid for. To corr.- ' bat this system, and open the eyes of the public, Worsley's of "The Pantheon," Gayt horn, Manchester, the Largest Retailers of Second-hand Furniture in the world, have determined to alow all respectable householders to have furniture at the ticketed marked cash price, a small percentage being charged for the conven'ence of payment by the week, month, or quarter. On the fourth page of the "Examiner" we advertise a list of Bargains every week, any of which can be had and paid for by convenient intervals, the articles being delivered upon the first payment being made. Furniture from £5 to £1 000 may be arranged for in this way. To give an idea of the equity of our proposals we are prepared to supply for a working man a House of Furniture complete, thoroughly good and substantial (every article warranted), price £2O cash down, not a penny less, or 10 per cent extra if credit is given, making £22. Full particulars sent on application. GREAT BARGAINS AT WORSLEY'S. ONE HUNDRED BEDROOM SUITES ON VIEW. Tl 4 Marvel: This is a solid American walnut Bedroom Suite, beautifully finished, has an excellent Wardrobe, with plate-glass centre door, Dressing-table, enclosed with latge drawers, the top fitted with large swing mirror and jewel drawers, Washstand, with Carrara marble slab and Minton tile back, with shaving glass, the bottom prettily enclosed with cupboard and drawers, Chairs, and Towel Rail com- plete; a very pretty suite. What price for tills, £25 or £3O? No, only 9 guineas. Money returned if not satisfactory. Ten per cent extra if credit is given. W. H. WORSLEY, "The Pantheon," Gaythnrn, Manchester. IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT. - . MONDAY. In the House of Commons nn Monday evening, Sir E. GREY, questioned by Mr. J. W. Lowther as to the diffi- culties inAfrica,sai I the French Government had been in- formed that Her Majesty's Government would be ready to enter with the French Government into a general review of all African questions pending between the two Gov- ernments for the purpose of such an adjustment as would place the relations of the two countries in Africa on a more satisfactory footing. Mr. .7. Mon.LEY, in answering a question put by Mr.T. W. Russell as to a meeting recently held in Virexford for the alleged purpose of denouncing "land-grabbers," said it was the opinion of the most competent advisers of tbe Government that to take notiee of every meeting and to hunt for cases of this kind was the way to increase dis- order in Ireland. Captain NAYLOR-LEYLAND moved the adjournment of , the House in order to call attention to the recernmenda- tions made by Mr. Pringle, a sub-commissioner of the Royal Commission on Agriculture, in eeeard to the county of Essex, and to ask the Government if they in- tended to give legislative effect to those recommenda- tions. Among other things he complained that the tithe and the land tax were excessive, and suggested that grants should he made from the Imperial Exchequer in aid of the local rates of the county. Sir W. Helicons:a. said the House should wait for the report of the Royal Commission, and Mr. H. GARDNE It pointed out that Mr. Pringle's report was merely evidence placed before the Commission. Mr. CNA PUN/ urged that Parliament should do something to arrest the fall in prices. The motion for the adjlurnment of the House %Yea defeated by 205 to 163. The House Lenin went into Committee on the Budget Bill. On clause 4, imposing an additional 1 per cent. duty on settled property,Mr.Bterenerzenoved an amend- ment to except ordinary cases of marriage settlement. The amendment was adopted, and Sir W. Hancouter offered to accept at a later stage an amendment by Mr. ^ Bartley to exempt settlements made before the passing of the bill. Other amendments were rejected by majo- rities of 31 and 51. The clause was added to the bill before the House rose. In the House of Lords the Eerl of DENBIGH moved the second readine of a bill to amend the Merchandise Marks Act, 1887. cio as to give powers to prevent the fraudulent marking of imported goods, and to deal with the practice of importing prison-it-lade goods. Lord BLAYPA IR opposed the hill as being unworkable, and the Lord Chancellor and the Earl of Kimberley also spoke against it. The Marquis of Salisbury sunpotted the motion, and on a division the second reading was car- ried by 49 to 26. TUESDAY. In the House of Ccmmons on Tuesday ereninte,Mr. H. Gardner informed Mr. Chaplin that he proposed to , held a special inquiry as to the character of the disease lof the infected cattle from Cenada, and Sir H. James and Dr. Sanderson, Waynflete Profeseor of Physiology at Oxford, had undertaken to act as legal and pathological assessors. In reply to questions, Sir W. FIATICOURT said that before the Budget Bill left the House the Government were perfectly prepared to ceme to a thoroughly good understanding with the colonies on the subject of the , estate duty. The House again went into Committee on the Budget Bill, resuming consideration of clause 5, which relates to the collection and recovery of the estate duty. The Points discussed were of a technical character. The Government majorities in the divisions ranged from 43 to 57. WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday, in Com- mittee of Supply on the Army Estimates,a discussion took place on the vote for provisions and forage. Reference was made to the gymnastic training of the men,to billet- ing, and to the importance of new ranges. Mr. Tom- LINSON said they had an admirable range at Chippine, but he urged that a light railway sheltie] be constructed to it. Mr. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN in reply admitted the necessity for new ranges, but said there was great diffi- culty in getting them. The suggestion with regard te the light railway to Chipping would he carefully con- sidered. The military authorities were constantly look- ing out for new sites, and were doing the beet they could with the limited means at their disposal. The health of the army and the repeal of the Contagions Diseases Acts were among the subjects discussed. Mr. CAMPBELL- BANNERMAN promised a Select Committee to inquire into the employment of meritorious soldiers, and it was sub- sequently appointed. The vote was agreed to. Another vote was talked out. Several bills were postponed. and , the House adjourned at twenty minutes to six o'clock.

Some of these Prince Brewers own many hundreds of ,of drinking houses, to the disquietude of otherwise peaceful localities, and the ruin of those whose money they are continually receiving without any inquiry as to the obligations, domestic and social, of the ciriukers.
